///Gangrene — 

Definition

Gangrene is the death and decomposition of body tissue caused by loss of blood supply, infection, or a combination of both. It commonly affects the extremities but may occur in other organs.

Etiology / Causes

1. Reduced blood supply
    * Atherosclerosis
    * Peripheral arterial disease
    * Thrombosis or embolism
2. Infection
    * Severe bacterial infection
3. Trauma
    * Crush injuries
    * Severe burns
4. Diabetes mellitus
5. Pressure and tissue ischemia
6. Frostbite

Types of Gangrene

1. Dry gangrene

* Usually caused by progressive loss of arterial blood supply.
* Tissue becomes dry, shriveled and black.
* Usually develops slowly.
* There is a clear line of demarcation between healthy and dead tissue.
* Infection is initially minimal.

2. Wet gangrene

* Occurs when ischemic tissue becomes infected.
* Tissue is swollen, soft and discolored.
* Pus and foul-smelling discharge may be present.
* Spreads rapidly.
* Can lead to sepsis and is a medical emergency.

3. Gas gangrene

* A severe infection of muscle and other tissues, commonly caused by Clostridium species.
* Produces gas within tissues.
* Severe pain, swelling and systemic toxicity may occur.
* Requires urgent medical and surgical treatment.

Clinical Features

* Severe pain in early stages or loss of sensation in advanced disease
* Skin discoloration: pale, blue, purple or black
* Swelling, particularly in wet gangrene
* Coldness of affected part
* Loss of peripheral pulse in arterial gangrene
* Skin ulceration
* Foul-smelling discharge in wet gangrene
* Fever and malaise
* Crepitus may occur in gas gangrene
* In severe cases, shock and sepsis

Complications

* Secondary infection
* Tissue necrosis
* Sepsis
* Septic shock
* Amputation
* Contractures and muscle weakness
* Loss of functional independence
* Death if untreated

Management

Medical management

* Treatment of the underlying cause
* Antibiotics for infected/wet or gas gangrene
* Control of diabetes and other risk factors
* Fluid and electrolyte management
* Pain management
* Improvement of blood supply when possible

Surgical management

* Debridement of dead tissue
* Revascularization when appropriate
* Incision and drainage of infected areas
* Amputation when tissue is non-viable or infection cannot otherwise be controlled

Physiotherapy Management

1. During acute stage

* Physiotherapy should be modified according to the patient’s medical condition.
* Avoid pressure, massage and unnecessary trauma to the affected tissue.
* Proper positioning of the affected limb.
* Maintain ROM of unaffected joints.
* Monitor vital signs and general condition.

2. After surgical treatment/amputation

* Prevent joint contractures through appropriate positioning and ROM exercises.
* Strengthen the remaining musculature.
* Improve balance and coordination.
* Bed mobility and transfer training.
* Gait training with appropriate assistive devices.
* Pre-prosthetic and prosthetic rehabilitation following amputation.
* Improve cardiovascular endurance and functional independence.
* Patient education regarding skin care, pressure relief and prevention of recurrence.

Physiotherapy Goals

* Prevent secondary complications.
* Maintain ROM and muscle strength.
* Prevent contractures.
* Promote mobility and independence.
* Improve gait and functional activities.
* Facilitate rehabilitation after amputation./////
</USER_REQUEST>

<USER_REQUEST>
Tuberculosis (TB)—

Definition

Tuberculosis (TB) is a chronic infectious disease caused mainly by Mycobacterium tuberculosis. It most commonly affects the lungs (pulmonary TB) but can involve other organs such as lymph nodes, bones, joints, kidneys and the central nervous system.

⸻

1. Etiological Agent

* Causative organism: Mycobacterium tuberculosis
* It is an acid-fast bacillus (AFB).
* It is transmitted mainly through airborne respiratory droplets/aerosols from a person with infectious pulmonary or laryngeal TB.

⸻

2. Risk Factors

* Close contact with an infectious TB patient
* Malnutrition
* Overcrowding
* Poor socioeconomic conditions
* Diabetes mellitus
* Immunosuppression
* HIV infection
* Smoking
* Substance abuse
* Poor general health

⸻

3. Pathophysiology

Inhalation of TB bacilli → bacilli reach alveoli → uptake by macrophages → cell-mediated immune response → granuloma/tubercle formation → latent or active infection.

In active disease, progressive tissue destruction may occur, particularly in the lungs, and infected individuals may transmit the organism to others.

⸻

4. Clinical Features

General symptoms

* Low-grade fever
* Night sweats
* Weight loss
* Loss of appetite
* Fatigue and weakness
* Malaise

Pulmonary symptoms

* Persistent cough
* Sputum production
* Chest pain
* Breathlessness
* Hemoptysis in some cases

Extrapulmonary TB

Clinical features depend on the organ involved.

Examples:

* Spinal TB: back pain, stiffness and neurological deficits
* Joint TB: pain, swelling and restricted movement
* TB meningitis: headache, vomiting and neurological manifestations

⸻

5. Investigations

1. Sputum examination for AFB
2. Rapid molecular tests such as nucleic acid amplification tests
3. Chest X-ray
4. CT scan when indicated
5. Tuberculin skin test / Mantoux test
6. Interferon-gamma release assay (IGRA)
7. Culture and drug-susceptibility testing
8. Blood investigations as re
<truncated 35413 bytes>
alance training
* ADL training
* Progressive endurance exercises
* Return to work and recreational activities

⸻

6. Precautions for Physiotherapists

Physiotherapy should be modified when there is:

* Active wound infection
* Wound dehiscence
* Excessive bleeding
* Uncontrolled pain
* Significant wound discharge
* Severe swelling
* Unstable vital signs
* Suspected DVT or systemic infection

Avoid:

* Direct aggressive pressure over an unstable wound
* Vigorous massage over infected tissue
* Excessive stretching that places stress on a healing surgical wound
* Exercise beyond the surgeon’s prescribed restrictions

Any unexpected deterioration in wound condition should be reported to the appropriate medical/wound-care team.

⸻

7. Physiotherapy Goals

The major goals are:

1. Promote safe functional recovery.
2. Maintain ROM.
3. Prevent contractures.
4. Maintain muscle strength.
5. Reduce complications of immobility.
6. Control edema where appropriate.
7. Improve mobility and gait.
8. Prevent scar-related functional restriction.
9. Improve independence in ADLs.
10. Educate the patient regarding wound protection and activity progression.

⸻

Flowchart for Examination

Tissue injury
↓
Hemostasis
Vasoconstriction → platelet aggregation → fibrin clot
↓
Inflammation
Neutrophils → macrophages → removal of bacteria/debris
↓
Proliferation
Fibroblasts → collagen → granulation tissue → angiogenesis → epithelialization → contraction
↓
Remodeling/Maturation
Collagen reorganization → scar maturation → increased tensile strength/////////////////////////////                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         
</USER_REQUEST>

<USER_REQUEST>
Abdominal Surgery: Incisions, Common Procedures and Physiotherapy Implications—-

Definition of Abdominal Surgery

Abdominal surgery refers to surgical procedures performed to diagnose, treat, repair, remove or reconstruct structures within the abdominal and pelvic cavities, including the gastrointestinal, hepatobiliary, urinary and reproductive organs.

An open abdominal operation (laparotomy) involves an incision through the abdominal wall to access the abdominal cavity, whereas laparoscopic surgery uses several small incisions and a camera. 

⸻

1. Common Abdominal Incisions

The choice of incision depends on the organ involved, required surgical exposure, urgency of surgery and patient factors. 

A. Midline incision

* Vertical incision through the linea alba.
* May be upper, lower or full midline.
* Provides rapid and wide access to the abdominal cavity.
* Commonly used in emergency and exploratory laparotomy.
* Easily extended if greater exposure is required. 

Physiotherapy relevance:
Large midline wounds may cause pain during coughing, deep breathing and movement. Patients require wound support, breathing exercises and gradual mobilization.

B. Paramedian incision

* Vertical incision placed lateral to the midline.
* May provide access to selected abdominal organs.
* Less commonly used today than the midline approach. 

C. Transverse incision

* Horizontal incision across the abdominal wall.
* May provide good cosmetic results and can follow the direction of abdominal wall structures.
* Used for selected abdominal procedures. 

D. Pfannenstiel incision

* Curved transverse incision just above the pubic symphysis.
* Commonly used for pelvic and gynaecological surgery, including abdominal hysterectomy.
* Provides good cosmetic results and relatively limited upper-abdominal access. 

E. Subcostal / Kocher incision

* Oblique incision below and parallel to the costal margin.
* Right subcostal: commonly used for gallbladder and liver surgery.
* 
<truncated 33497 bytes>
matic gallstones (cholelithiasis).
* Acute cholecystitis.
* Chronic cholecystitis.
* Biliary dyskinesia.
* Acalculous cholecystitis.
* Gallstone pancreatitis.
* Selected gallbladder polyps or masses.
* Complications of gallstone disease. 

Surgical Steps of Laparoscopic Cholecystectomy

1. Pre-operative preparation
    * Patient assessment and consent.
    * Fasting as instructed.
    * General anesthesia.
2. Positioning
    * Patient is positioned appropriately.
    * Abdomen is prepared and draped.
3. Creation of pneumoperitoneum
    * Carbon dioxide is introduced into the abdominal cavity.
4. Insertion of ports
    * Laparoscope and surgical instruments are introduced through small abdominal incisions.
5. Exposure of gallbladder
    * Gallbladder is grasped and retracted to expose the hepatocystic/Calot triangle.
6. Identification of structures
    * Cystic duct and cystic artery are clearly identified.
    * The critical view of safety is obtained before division.
7. Division of cystic duct and artery
    * Structures are clipped/ligated and divided.
8. Dissection of gallbladder
    * Gallbladder is separated from the liver bed.
9. Removal
    * Gallbladder is placed in a retrieval bag and removed through a port.
10. Inspection and closure

* Hemostasis is checked.
* Abdomen is desufflated.
* Ports are removed.
* Incisions are closed. 

Open Cholecystectomy

* Right upper abdominal incision is made, commonly a right subcostal/Kocher incision.
* Gallbladder is exposed.
* Cystic duct and cystic artery are ligated and divided.
* Gallbladder is separated from the liver.
* Gallbladder is removed.
* Wound is closed. 

Flowchart

Anesthesia → Pneumoperitoneum → Ports → Expose gallbladder → Identify cystic duct & artery → Clip/divide → Separate gallbladder from liver → Remove gallbladder → Hemostasis → Closure///////

Abdominal Surgery Incisions Common Procedures and Physiotherapy Implications --- **Definition of Abdominal Surgery** Abdominal surgery refers to surgical procedures performed to diagnose treat repair remove or reconstruct structures within the abdominal and pelvic cavities including the gastrointestinal hepatobiliary urinary and reproductive organs An open abdominal operation laparotomy involves an incision through the abdominal wall to access the abdominal cavity whereas laparoscopic surgery uses several small incisions and a camera --- **1 Common Abdominal Incisions** The choice of incision depends on the organ involved required surgical exposure urgency of surgery and patient factors **A Midline incision** • Vertical incision through the linea alba • May be upper lower or full midline • Provides rapid and wide access to the abdominal cavity • Commonly used in emergency and exploratory laparotomy • Easily extended if greater exposure is required **Physiotherapy relevance** Large midline wounds may cause pain during coughing deep breathing and movement Patients require wound support breathing exercises and gradual mobilization **B Paramedian incision** • Vertical incision placed lateral to the midline • May provide access to selected abdominal organs • Less commonly used today than the midline approach **C Transverse incision** • Horizontal incision across the abdominal wall • May provide good cosmetic results and can follow the direction of abdominal wall structures • Used for selected abdominal procedures **D Pfannenstiel incision** • Curved transverse incision just above the pubic symphysis • Commonly used for pelvic and gynaecological surgery including abdominal hysterectomy • Provides good cosmetic results and relatively limited upper abdominal access **E Subcostal / Kocher incision** • Oblique incision below and parallel to the costal margin • Right subcostal commonly used for gallbladder and liver surgery • Left subcostal may be used for splenic surgery • Bilateral subc

<USER_REQUEST>
1. DVT – Deep Vein Thrombosis

* Definition: Formation of a blood clot (thrombus) in a deep vein, usually of the lower limb.
* Signs: Unilateral swelling, calf pain/tenderness, warmth and redness.
* Complication: Pulmonary embolism.
* Prevention: Early mobilization, ankle-pump exercises, compression devices and anticoagulants when prescribed.

⸻

2. Scar + Types

* Definition: A scar is fibrous connective tissue formed during healing after tissue injury.
* Types:
    * Normal scar: Flat, soft and within wound margins.
    * Hypertrophic scar: Raised but remains within wound margins.
    * Keloid: Raised scar extending beyond original wound margins.
    * Atrophic scar: Thin, depressed scar.

⸻

3. Types of Antibiotics

* Cell-wall inhibitors: Penicillins, cephalosporins.
* Protein-synthesis inhibitors: Aminoglycosides, macrolides, tetracyclines.
* Nucleic-acid inhibitors: Fluoroquinolones, rifampicin.
* Antimetabolites: Sulfonamides, trimethoprim.
* Cell-membrane disruptors: Polymyxins.

⸻

4. Dehydration + Manifestations

* Definition: Dehydration is a condition in which body water loss exceeds water intake.
* Manifestations:
    * Thirst.
    * Dry mouth and mucous membranes.
    * Reduced/dark urine.
    * Weakness and dizziness.
    * Tachycardia.
    * Hypotension in severe cases.

⸻

5. Surgical Drains

* Definition: Surgical drains are tubes/devices used to remove blood, pus, serous fluid or other unwanted collections from a wound or body cavity.
* Examples:
    * Intercostal drain (ICD).
    * Hemovac.
    * Jackson-Pratt drain.
    * T-tube.
* Purpose: Prevent fluid accumulation and facilitate healing.

⸻

6. Types of Anaesthesia

* Local anaesthesia: Loss of sensation in a limited area without loss of consciousness.
* Regional anaesthesia: Blocks sensation in a larger region.
    * Example: spinal, epidural.
* General anaesthesia: Drug-induced reversible unconsciousness with loss of protective reflexes and sensation.
<truncated 79224 bytes>
ain perceived in the missing limb.
* Features: Burning, tingling, shooting pain or sensation of movement.
* Management: Desensitization, mirror therapy, graded motor imagery, appropriate prosthetic rehabilitation and medical pain management.

⸻

14. Varicose Veins

* Definition: Varicose veins are dilated, elongated and tortuous superficial veins, usually of the lower limbs, caused mainly by venous valve incompetence.
* Features: Visible twisted veins, aching, heaviness, ankle edema and skin changes.
* Management: Exercise, leg elevation, compression when appropriate and selected surgical/endovenous treatment.

⸻

15. Craniotomy

* Definition: Craniotomy is a neurosurgical procedure in which a portion of the skull is temporarily removed to provide access to the brain and is usually replaced after surgery.
* Indications: Brain tumour, aneurysm, hematoma, vascular lesions and other intracranial conditions.
* Difference: In craniectomy, the bone flap is not immediately replaced.

⸻

16. Laminectomy

* Definition: Laminectomy is a spinal operation involving removal of part or all of the vertebral lamina to decompress the spinal cord or nerve roots.
* Indications: Spinal stenosis, nerve-root compression, selected disc disease and spinal tumours.
* Complications: Dural tear/CSF leak, infection, bleeding, neurological injury and spinal instability. 18. Postoperative Complications

* Definition: Postoperative complications are unwanted conditions occurring after surgery that may delay recovery or cause morbidity.
* Common complications:
    * Shock.
    * DVT.
    * Wound infection.
    * Atelectasis.
    * Pneumonia.
    * UTI.
    * Pressure injuries.
    * Hemorrhage.
    * Wound dehiscence.
    * Ileus.
* Physiotherapy prevention: Early mobilization, breathing exercises, circulation exercises, ROM, positioning and functional rehabilitation.///////////////////

5. Surgical Drains

    * T-tube.

6. Types of Anaesthesia

<truncated 72738 bytes>
 * Examples:
        * Cellulitis
        * Abscess
        * Acute wound infection
        * Necrotizing soft-tissue infection
* Chronic surgical infections
    * Persist for a prolonged period.
    * May cause fibrosis, sinus formation or tissue destruction.
    * Examples:
        * Tuberculosis
        * Chronic osteomyelitis
        * Chronic wound infection

⸻

2. According to Site

* Superficial infections
    * Involve skin and subcutaneous tissue.
    * Examples: cellulitis, superficial wound infection.
* Deep infections
    * Involve deep fascia, muscle or deeper tissues.
    * Examples: deep incisional surgical-site infection, deep abscess.
* Organ/space infections
    * Involve an organ or body space related to surgery.
    * Examples:
        * Intra-abdominal abscess
        * Pelvic abscess
        * Postoperative pneumonia.

⸻

3. According to Source

* Exogenous infection
    * Organisms enter from outside the patient’s body.
    * Example: contamination from instruments, environment or healthcare personnel.
* Endogenous infection
    * Caused by microorganisms already present in the patient’s own body.
    * Example: infection caused by intestinal flora following bowel surgery.

⸻

4. Surgical Site Infection (SSI) Classification

* Superficial incisional SSI
    * Involves skin and subcutaneous tissue.
* Deep incisional SSI
    * Involves deep soft tissues such as fascia and muscle.
* Organ/space SSI
    * Involves any part of the body deeper than the fascia/muscle that was opened or manipulated during surgery. (cdc.gov￼)

⸻

5. According to Presence of Pus

* Non-suppurative infection
    * Does not primarily produce pus.
    * Example: cellulitis.
* Suppurative infection
    * Characterized by pus formation.
    * Examples:
        * Abscess
        * Empyema
        * Infected wound.////////////////////

urgical infection is an infection involving a wound tissue or organ that occurs before during or after a surgical procedure and may interfere with normal healing --- **Classification of Surgical Infections** **1 According to Clinical Course** • Acute surgical infections • Develop rapidly • Usually associated with acute inflammation • Examples • Cellulitis • Abscess • Acute wound infection • Necrotizing soft tissue infection • Chronic surgical infections • Persist for a prolonged period • May cause fibrosis sinus formation or tissue destruction • Examples • Tuberculosis • Chronic osteomyelitis • Chronic wound infection --- **2 According to Site** • Superficial infections • Involve skin and subcutaneous tissue • Examples cellulitis superficial wound infection • Deep infections • Involve deep fascia muscle or deeper tissues • Examples deep incisional surgical site infection deep abscess • Organ/space infections • Involve an organ or body space related to surgery • Examples • Intra abdominal abscess • Pelvic abscess • Postoperative pneumonia --- **3 According to Source** • Exogenous infection • Organisms enter from outside the patients body • Example contamination from instruments environment or healthcare personnel • Endogenous infection • Caused by microorganisms already present in the patients own body • Example infection caused by intestinal flora following bowel surgery --- **4 Surgical Site Infection SSI Classification** • Superficial incisional SSI • Involves skin and subcutaneous tissue • Deep incisional SSI • Involves deep soft tissues such as fascia and muscle • Organ/space SSI • Involves any part of the body deeper than the fascia/muscle that was opened or manipulated during surgery cdc gov --- **5 According to Presence of Pus** • Non suppurative infection • Does not primarily produce pus • Example cellulitis • Suppurative infection • Characterized by pus formation • Examples • Abscess • Empyema • Infected wound

Anesthesia

Types: General, Spinal and Local — Systemic Effects

1. Definition

* Anesthesia is a medically induced, temporary and usually reversible state in which sensation, especially pain, is reduced or abolished, with or without loss of consciousness, to permit surgical or diagnostic procedures. 

⸻

2. Types of Anesthesia

A. General Anesthesia

Definition

* General anesthesia is a drug-induced state of unconsciousness with loss of sensation and reduced protective reflexes during surgery. 

Features

* Patient is unconscious.
* Pain sensation is suppressed.
* Protective airway reflexes are reduced.
* Muscle relaxation may be produced.
* Airway support/ventilation may be required.

Uses

* Major abdominal surgery.
* Thoracic surgery.
* Neurosurgery.
* Major orthopaedic surgery.
* Procedures that require prolonged immobility.

⸻

B. Spinal Anesthesia

Definition

* Spinal anesthesia is a regional anesthetic technique in which a local anesthetic is injected into the subarachnoid/intrathecal space, producing temporary sensory and motor blockade. 

Features

* Patient is usually conscious.
* Produces numbness and weakness below the level of the block.
* Commonly used for lower abdominal, pelvic and lower-limb surgery. 

Common effects/complications

* Hypotension.
* Bradycardia.
* Urinary retention.
* Post-dural puncture headache.
* Temporary motor weakness.
* Rare neurological complications. 

⸻

C. Local Anesthesia

Definition

* Local anesthesia produces temporary loss of sensation in a specific, limited area without causing loss of consciousness. 

Examples

* Local infiltration for wound suturing.
* Dental anesthesia.
* Minor skin procedures.
* Peripheral nerve blocks.

Mechanism

* Local anesthetics block voltage-gated sodium channels, preventing nerve impulse transmission. 

⸻

3. Systemic Effects of Anesthesia

A. Respiratory System

* Respiratory depression, particularly with general anesthesia an
<truncated 15811 bytes>
hria.
* Ataxia.
* Balance and gait disturbance.
* Weakness.
* Sensory disturbance.
* Cognitive or neurological deficits depending on the target.
* Hemorrhage or infection with invasive procedures.

⸻

5. Neural Implants

Definition

* Neural implants are medical devices implanted in or near the nervous system to record neural activity or deliver electrical stimulation to modify nervous-system function.

Examples

* Deep brain stimulators.
* Vagus nerve stimulators.
* Responsive neurostimulation systems.
* Spinal cord stimulators.
* Cochlear implants.

Indications

* Parkinson’s disease.
* Essential tremor.
* Epilepsy.
* Chronic neuropathic pain.
* Hearing loss.
* Selected movement disorders.
* Selected psychiatric or neurological conditions.

Complications

* Infection.
* Bleeding.
* Device malfunction.
* Lead migration or breakage.
* Pain at the implantation site.
* Nerve or brain injury.
* Stimulation-related adverse effects. ////////////Radiological Interpretation of OA and RA


1. Osteoarthritis (OA)

* OA is a degenerative joint disease characterized by progressive cartilage loss and changes in the subchondral bone.
* X-ray findings:
    * Non-uniform joint-space narrowing.
    * Osteophytes (marginal bony outgrowths).
    * Subchondral sclerosis.
    * Subchondral cysts.
    * Bony remodeling/deformity in advanced disease.
* Usually affects weight-bearing joints such as the knee and hip.
* Often has an asymmetrical distribution.

2. Rheumatoid Arthritis (RA)

* RA is a chronic autoimmune inflammatory disease affecting the synovial joints.
* X-ray findings:
    * Symmetrical joint-space narrowing.
    * Marginal bone erosions.
    * Periarticular osteopenia.
    * Soft-tissue swelling.
    * Subluxation and deformity in advanced disease.
* Commonly affects MCP, PIP and wrist joints; DIP joints are usually spared.//// /////////

Fractures of the Upper limb —
General causes

* Fall on an outstretched hand (FOOSH)
* Direct blow or trauma
* Road-traffic accidents
* Sports injuries
* Twisting or rotational injuries
* Occupational injuries
* Osteoporosis/pathological bone weakness

General clinical features

* Pain and tenderness
* Swelling and bruising
* Deformity or abnormal movement
* Loss of function and restricted ROM
* Crepitus
* Shortening or abnormal alignment
* Neurovascular deficit in complicated fractures

General complications

* Neurovascular injury
* Compartment syndrome
* Infection in open fractures
* Delayed union/non-union
* Malunion
* Joint stiffness
* Post-traumatic arthritis
* Complex regional pain syndrome

⸻

1. Fractures of Clavicle and Scapula

A. Fracture of clavicle

Definition:
Fracture of the clavicle, most commonly involving the middle third.

Causes

* Fall directly on the shoulder
* Fall on an outstretched hand
* Direct blow to clavicle
* Road-traffic or sports injury

Clinical features

* Pain and tenderness over clavicle
* Swelling and deformity
* Patient supports the affected arm
* Shoulder drooping forwards and downwards
* Abnormal mobility/crepitus
* Difficulty in shoulder movements
* Check distal neurovascular status

Complications

* Malunion
* Non-union
* Neurovascular injury
* Skin perforation
* Brachial plexus injury
* Pneumothorax
* Shoulder stiffness

Conservative management

* Arm sling or figure-of-eight brace
* Analgesics
* Ice/elevation initially
* Early elbow, wrist and hand movements
* Gradual shoulder ROM after pain subsides

Surgical management

* ORIF with plate and screws
* Indications: open fracture, neurovascular injury, significant displacement/shortening, threatened skin, or selected unstable displaced fractures.

B. Fracture of scapula

Causes

* Usually high-energy trauma
* Direct blow
* RTA
* Fall from height
* Indirect injury through shoulder

Clinical features

* Severe shoulder/scapular pain
* Swelling and tenderness
* Painful shoulder movements
* Reduced should
<truncated 78120 bytes>
njuries above approximately T6. 

⸻

7. Clinical Patterns of Incomplete SCI

A. Central Cord Syndrome

* Usually follows cervical hyperextension injury.
* Upper limbs are affected more than lower limbs.
* Hand weakness may be prominent.
* Sensory disturbance may occur.

B. Brown-Séquard Syndrome

Due to hemisection of the spinal cord:

* Ipsilateral motor weakness
* Ipsilateral loss of proprioception/vibration
* Contralateral loss of pain and temperature

C. Anterior Cord Syndrome

* Motor paralysis below the lesion
* Loss of pain and temperature
* Relative preservation of dorsal-column functions such as vibration and proprioception. 

⸻

8. Complete and Incomplete Injury

Complete SCI

* No sensory or motor function is preserved in the sacral segments S4–S5.
* No sacral sparing.
* Classified as AIS/ASIA Grade A. 

Incomplete SCI

* Some sensory and/or motor function is preserved below the neurological level, including sacral function.
* Classified as AIS B–D depending on the amount of preserved function.
* AIS E: normal motor and sensory examination. 

⸻

9. Clinical Examination

Assessment should include:

* Level of neurological injury
* Motor power
* Sensory examination
* Deep tendon reflexes
* Anal sensation and voluntary anal contraction
* Muscle tone
* Respiratory function
* Bladder and bowel function
* Autonomic function

The International Standards for Neurological Classification of Spinal Cord Injury (ISNCSCI/ASIA examination) is used to determine the neurological level and severity of injury. 

⸻

10. Important Complications

* Respiratory failure
* Neurogenic shock
* Spinal shock
* Pressure sores
* Deep-vein thrombosis and pulmonary embolism
* Urinary tract infection
* Neurogenic bladder and bowel
* Contractures
* Spasticity
* Autonomic dysreflexia
* Chronic pain
* Permanent paralysis/////////////////

Fractures of Thoracic and Lumbar Spine —

Definition

A thoracic or lumbar spine fracture is a break in one or more vertebral structures of the thoracic or lumbar spine, with or without associated ligament, spinal cord or nerve-root injury.

Thoracolumbar fractures commonly occur around the T11-L2 region, which represents the transition between the relatively rigid thoracic spine and mobile lumbar spine. 

⸻

1. Mechanism of Injury

Thoracolumbar fractures commonly result from:

1. Flexion-compression

* Sudden forward flexion of the spine.
* Produces compression/wedge fractures.
* Common after falls and road-traffic accidents.

2. Axial compression

* Vertical force transmitted through the spine.
* Produces burst fractures with compression of the vertebral body and po…
FRACTURE OF RIB CAGE —

Definition

A fracture of the rib cage is a break in the continuity of one or more ribs or the sternum, usually caused by direct or indirect trauma. Severe chest trauma may be associated with pneumothorax, haemothorax, pulmonary contusion or flail chest.

⸻

1. FRACTURE OF RIBS

Mechanism of Injury

* Direct trauma: A blow to the chest causes the rib to bend and fracture at the site of impact.
* Indirect trauma: Compression of the chest from the sides may fracture ribs at their point of maximum curvature.
* RTA
* Fall
* Sports injury
* Crush injury
* Severe coughing may rarely cause stress fractures.

Clinical Features

* Severe localized chest pain
* Pain aggravated by:
    * Deep inspiration
    * Coughing
    * Sneezing
    * Movement
* Local …
INFLAMMATION AND REPAIR —-

Introduction to Soft-Tissue Healing

Definition of Inflammation

Inflammation is a protective response of living vascularized tissue to injury, infection or irritation, which removes the injurious stimulus and initiates the process of healing and repair.

⸻

1. Causes of Inflammation

* Trauma and mechanical injury
* Burns
* Infection
* Chemical irritants
* Foreign bodies
* Tissue necrosis
* Ischaemia
* Immune reactions

AMPUTATIONS

Definition and Levels of Amputation
—
Definition

Amputation is the partial or complete removal of a limb or part of a limb through a surgical procedure or as a result of trauma.

The main aims of amputation are to remove non-viable or diseased tissue, control infection or pain, and create a functional residual limb suitable for rehabilitation and prosthetic fitting.

⸻

1. Levels of Upper-Limb Amputation

A. Finger and Hand

1. Distal phalanx amputation
2. Middle phalanx amputation
3. Proximal phalanx amputation
4. Metacarpal amputation
5. Ray amputation – removal of a digit with its corresponding metacarpal.
6. Transmetacarpal amputation – through the metacarpals.

B. Wrist and Forearm

7. Wrist disarticulation – separation through the wrist joint.
8. Transradi…
Traumatic Spinal Cord Injuries—

Definition

Traumatic spinal cord injury (SCI) is an acute injury to the spinal cord caused by an external mechanical force, resulting in temporary or permanent impairment of motor, sensory and autonomic functions below the level of injury. 

⸻

Causes

* Road-traffic accidents
* Falls from height
* Sports injuries, especially diving
* Direct blow to the spine
* Gunshot or penetrating injuries
* Crush injuries
* Hyperflexion or hyperextension injuries
* Fracture-dislocation of the vertebral column

⸻

Clinical Features

Clinical presentation depends mainly on the level, severity and completeness of spinal cord injury.

* Muscle weakness or paralysis below the level of injury

Medical & Allied Health Sciences: High-Yield Revision
Comprehensive 45-Question Master Bank (02-Mark Definitions, Features & Classifications)
45 EXAM QUESTIONS 2-MARK STANDARD FORMAT RESPIRATORY • CVS • ICU • DERMATOLOGY • OB-GYN
PART I: RESPIRATORY PATHOLOGY & PULMONARY DISORDERS
01 Bronchial Asthma
Definition:A chronic inflammatory disorder of the airways characterized by bronchial hyperresponsiveness, chronic
inflammation, and recurrent episodes of reversible airflow obstruction and bronchospasm.
Common Triggers:Inhaled allergens (pollen, dust mites, animal dander), viral respiratory tract infections, cold ambient air,
physical exertion, atmospheric smoke/pollutants, and medications (aspirin, non-selective beta-blockers).
02 Chronic Obstructive Pulmonary Disease (COPD)
Definition:A common, preventable, and treatable pulmonary disease characterized by persistent, usually progressive airflow
limitation caused by a combination of small airway disease (obstructive bronchiolitis) and parenchymal destruction
(emphysema).
Major Risk Factors:Tobacco cigarette smoking (primary risk factor), exposure to biomass fuel smoke and poorly ventilated
indoor cooking, occupational dusts and chemical fumes, ambient air pollution, and genetic α1-antitrypsin deficiency.
03 Tuberculosis (TB)
Definition:A chronic, communicable granulomatous bacterial infection caused by Mycobacterium tuberculosis, primarily
affecting the pulmonary parenchyma but capable of hematogenous or lymphatic dissemination to extrapulmonary organs.
Common Modes of Transmission:Inhalation of microscopic airborne droplet nuclei (1 to 5 μm in diameter) expelled when a
patient with active pulmonary or laryngeal tuberculosis coughs, sneezes, speaks, or sings.
04 Bronchiectasis
Definition:A chronic pulmonary disorder characterized by permanent, abnormal dilation and destruction of the elastic and
muscular components of the bronchial walls, driven by a vicious cycle of recurrent infection and transmural inflammation.
Com
<truncated 22232 bytes>
ettage (D&C)
Definition:A minor gynecological operative procedure involving the gradual mechanical enlargement/dilatation of the cervical
canal followed by the scraping or vacuum evacuation (curettage) of the endometrial lining.
Key Indications:
Diagnostic: Evaluation of postmenopausal bleeding, abnormal uterine bleeding, or suspected endometrial hyperplasia and
neoplasia.
Therapeutic: Evacuation of retained products of conception following incomplete or missed abortion, management of acute
heavy uterine bleeding, or polyp excision.
44 Pre-eclampsia
Definition:A multisystem disorder of pregnancy characterized by the new development of arterial hypertension (≥ 140/90
mmHg) presenting after 20 weeks of gestation in a previously normotensive female, accompanied by proteinuria and/or
maternal organ dysfunction.
Major Clinical Features:Persistent gestational hypertension, significant proteinuria (≥ 300 mg/24 h), rapid pathological facial
and non-dependent hand edema, throbbing frontal headache, scotomata and visual blurriness, epigastric or right upper
quadrant abdominal tenderness, and thrombocytopenia.
45 Sexually Transmitted Diseases (STDs)
Definition:A heterogeneous group of communicable infectious syndromes caused by bacteria, viruses, parasites, or fungi that
are predominantly spread from person to person through unprotected sexual intercourse (vaginal, anal, or oral).
Two Classical Examples:
Syphilis: A chronic systemic venereal infection caused by the spirochete Treponema pallidum, presenting primarily with an
indurated, painless chancre.
Gonorrhea: An acute purulent mucosal infection of the genitourinary tract caused by the Gram-negative diplococcus 
Neisseria gonorrhoeae.

Medical & Allied Health Sciences: High-Yield Revision
Comprehensive 45-Question Master Bank (02-Mark Definitions, Features & Classifications)
45 EXAM QUESTIONS 2-MARK STANDARD FORMAT RESPIRATORY • CVS • ICU • DERMATOLOGY • OB-GYN
PART I: RESPIRATORY PATHOLOGY & PULMONARY DISORDERS
01 Bronchial Asthma
Definition:A chronic inflammatory disorder of the airways characterized by bronchial hyperresponsiveness, chronic
inflammation, and recurrent episodes of reversible airflow obstruction and bronchospasm.
Common Triggers:Inhaled allergens (pollen, dust mites, animal dander), viral respiratory tract infections, cold ambient air,
physical exertion, atmospheric smoke/pollutants, and medications (aspirin, non-selective beta-blockers).
02 Chronic Obstructive Pulmonary Disease (COPD)
Definition:A common, preventable, and treatable pulmonary disease characterized by persistent, usually progressive airflow
limitation caused by a combination of small airway disease (obstructive bronchiolitis) and parenchymal destruction
(emphysema).
Major Risk Factors:Tobacco cigarette smoking (primary risk factor), exposure to biomass fuel smoke and poorly ventilated
indoor cooking, occupational dusts and chemical fumes, ambient air pollution, and genetic α1-antitrypsin deficiency.
03 Tuberculosis (TB)
Definition:A chronic, communicable granulomatous bacterial infection caused by Mycobacterium tuberculosis, primarily
affecting the pulmonary parenchyma but capable of hematogenous or lymphatic dissemination to extrapulmonary organs.
Common Modes of Transmission:Inhalation of microscopic airborne droplet nuclei (1 to 5 μm in diameter) expelled when a
patient with active pulmonary or laryngeal tuberculosis coughs, sneezes, speaks, or sings.
04 Bronchiectasis
Definition:A chronic pulmonary disorder characterized by permanent, abnormal dilation and destruction of the elastic and
muscular components of the bronchial walls, driven by a vicious cycle of recurrent infection and transmural inflammation.
Com
<truncated 22232 bytes>
ettage (D&C)
Definition:A minor gynecological operative procedure involving the gradual mechanical enlargement/dilatation of the cervical
canal followed by the scraping or vacuum evacuation (curettage) of the endometrial lining.
Key Indications:
Diagnostic: Evaluation of postmenopausal bleeding, abnormal uterine bleeding, or suspected endometrial hyperplasia and
neoplasia.
Therapeutic: Evacuation of retained products of conception following incomplete or missed abortion, management of acute
heavy uterine bleeding, or polyp excision.
44 Pre-eclampsia
Definition:A multisystem disorder of pregnancy characterized by the new development of arterial hypertension (≥ 140/90
mmHg) presenting after 20 weeks of gestation in a previously normotensive female, accompanied by proteinuria and/or
maternal organ dysfunction.
Major Clinical Features:Persistent gestational hypertension, significant proteinuria (≥ 300 mg/24 h), rapid pathological facial
and non-dependent hand edema, throbbing frontal headache, scotomata and visual blurriness, epigastric or right upper
quadrant abdominal tenderness, and thrombocytopenia.
45 Sexually Transmitted Diseases (STDs)
Definition:A heterogeneous group of communicable infectious syndromes caused by bacteria, viruses, parasites, or fungi that
are predominantly spread from person to person through unprotected sexual intercourse (vaginal, anal, or oral).
Two Classical Examples:
Syphilis: A chronic systemic venereal infection caused by the spirochete Treponema pallidum, presenting primarily with an
indurated, painless chancre.
Gonorrhea: An acute purulent mucosal infection of the genitourinary tract caused by the Gram-negative diplococcus 
Neisseria gonorrhoeae.

Medical & Allied Health Sciences: Master Examination Guide
Comprehensive 32-Question Master Bank (05-Mark Long-Form Model Answers)
QUESTIONS 01 – 32 5-MARK EXAMINATION FORMAT SYSTEMIC PATHOLOGY • CRITICAL CARE • DERMATOLOGY • OB-GYN
PART I: RESPIRATORY PATHOLOGY & PULMONARY MEDICINE (Q01 – Q07)
Q 01 Acute Bronchitis
Definition: An acute, self-limiting inflammation of the tracheobronchial mucosa presenting characteristically with persistent cough with or
without sputum production.
Etiology:
Viral (>90%): Influenza A/B, Parainfluenza, RSV, Rhinovirus, Adenovirus, Coronavirus.
Bacterial (<10%):Mycoplasma pneumoniae, Chlamydophila pneumoniae, Bordetella pertussis.
Non-Infectious: Chemical fumes, dusts, tobacco smoke, air pollutants.
Clinical Features: Persistent cough (initially dry/hacking, later mucoid or purulent, lasting 1–3 weeks); retrosternal burning soreness on
coughing; low-grade fever, malaise, myalgias; scattered expiratory wheezes/rhonchi and coarse crackles that clear upon coughing.
Investigations: Primarily clinical diagnosis. Chest X-ray (PA view) is usually normal; indicated to exclude pneumonia if tachypnea,
tachycardia, high fever, or consolidation signs are present. Sputum PCR only for suspected B. pertussis.
Complications: Secondary bronchopneumonia, acute asthma/COPD exacerbation, chronic post-infectious bronchial
hyperresponsiveness (>4 weeks).
Management: Supportive hydration and steam inhalation; Paracetamol/Ibuprofen for chest soreness and fever; Dextromethorphan for
disruptive dry cough; inhaled Salbutamol for bronchospasm/wheeze. Routine antibiotics are not recommended unless elderly with
comorbidities or confirmed pertussis (Azithromycin).
Q 02 Bronchial Pneumonia (Bronchopneumonia)
Definition: Acute suppurative inflammation of the pulmonary parenchyma centered around terminal and respiratory bronchioles,
presenting with patchy, multifocal consolidation involving multiple pulmonary lobes.
Causes: Bacterial: Streptococcus pneumoniae, Staphyl
<truncated 48333 bytes>
veries,
postmenopausal estrogen deficiency, obesity, chronic cough).
Urge Incontinence (UUI / Overactive Bladder, ~20%): Involuntary leakage preceded by an intense, sudden, uncontrollable urge to
void. Caused by detrusor overactivity (idiopathic, stroke, Parkinson's, MS, UTI, bladder stones).
Mixed Incontinence (~30%): Features of both stress and urge incontinence.
Overflow: Involuntary dribbling from a chronically overfilled, distended bladder due to bladder outlet obstruction (pelvic prolapse) or
hypotonic detrusor (diabetic neuropathy).
Functional: Inability to reach toilet due to severe physical or cognitive disability (dementia, severe arthritis).
Clinical Features: Leakage on strain/cough (SUI); urinary urgency, daytime frequency (>8 times), and nocturia (UUI); continuous
dribbling (overflow); perineal skin dermatitis, secondary UTIs, anxiety, and social withdrawal.
Management:
Conservative & Behavioral (First-line): Lifestyle: weight loss, fluid moderation, avoid caffeine/alcohol; Pelvic Floor Muscle
Training (Kegel exercises): at least 3 sets of 8–12 maximal contractions daily for ≥3 months (first-line for SUI); Bladder training
(timed voiding) for UUI.
Pharmacological: For UUI: Antimuscarinics (Oxybutynin, Tolterodine, Solifenacin) or β3-agonist (Mirabegron); Local topical vaginal
estrogen for postmenopausal women; SUI: Duloxetine (SNRI).
Interventional & Surgical: Intravesical OnabotulinumtoxinA injections or sacral neuromodulation for refractory UUI; Mid-Urethral
Sling (MUS: TVT / TOT) synthetic mesh placement (gold standard for refractory SUI); Burch colposuspension; Periurethral bulking
agents.

diameter and wall thickness of a hollow tube • Key Difference for Full Marks Tube drawing requires a mandrel inside the tube to support the internal walls and control the final internal diameter Wire **GTU Mid-Sem Master Cheat Sheet: Tier 1 & Easy Wins (UPDATED)**
This document contains exactly what you must write to get full marks for the highest-probability topics, including all necessary diagrams, advantages, and limitations required for 7-mark GTU questions.

**CHAPTER 1: FOUNDRY TECHNOLOGY**

**1. Six Applications of Casting (3 Marks):** Easy Win (Appeared in actual mid-sem). Write these exact 6 points: 1. Automotive engine blocks and cylinder heads. 2. Machine tool beds and frames (due to damping capacity). 3. Turbine blades (aerospace). 4. Railway wheels and tracks. 5. Pump housings and valve bodies. 6. Agricultural machinery components.

**2. Pattern Allowances - Theory (7 Marks):** Tier 1. What to write for full marks: • Definition: Intentional dimensional modifications made to a pattern to compensate for metal shrinkage, machining, and casting defects. • 1. Shrinkage Allowance: Metals shrink when cooling from liquid to solid. Pattern is made larger. (Values: CI = 10mm/m, Steel = 20mm/m, Al = 13mm/m). • 2. Machining/Finish Allowance: Extra material added to surfaces that require machining later. (Usually 1.5 to 6 mm). • 3. Draft Allowance: Taper applied to vertical faces of the pattern for easy withdrawal without damaging the mould. (1° to 3° external, 2° to 5° internal). • 4. Shake/Rapping Allowance: When a pattern is shaken to remove it, the mould cavity enlarges. Pattern is made slightly smaller to compensate. (Negative allowance). • 5. Distortion/Camber Allowance: Applied to U or V-shaped castings that tend to warp during cooling. Pattern is bent in the opposite direction. • Diagram Required (Crucial for 7M): Draw a 2D cross-section of a solid block. Show a dotted line outside for "Machining Allowance" and angled vertical sides for "Draft Allowance".

**3. Pattern Allowance - Numerical (7 Marks):** Tier 1 (From actual mid-sem). How to score full marks: 1. Read dimensions: Identify L, W, H of the final casting. 2. Add Shrinkage: Add the % or mm/m shrinkage to each dimension.
<truncated 5697 bytes>
sqrt{R \cdot \Delta t}$. 3. True Strain: $\epsilon = \ln(\frac{h_0}{h_1})$. 4. Average Flow Stress ($\bar{Y}_f$): If given directly, use it. (If given as $K\epsilon^n$, then $\bar{Y}_f = \frac{K\epsilon^n}{n+1}$). 5. Roll Force (F): $F = \bar{Y}_f \cdot w \cdot L_c$ (where w = width of plate). 6. Torque (T): $T = F \cdot a$ (where $a$ is the moment arm. Usually $a = 0.5 \cdot L_c$ for hot rolling). For ONE roll. 7. Total Power (P): $P = 2 \cdot \frac{2\pi N T}{60}$ (Multiply by 2 because there are two rolls).

**13. Extrusion: Forward vs Backward (7 Marks):** Tier 1. What to write for full marks: • Forward (Direct) Extrusion: Billet is placed in a chamber. Ram pushes the billet. The metal flows in the same direction as the ram movement. Sketch: Show ram pushing right, metal exiting right. Advantage: Simpler tooling. Disadvantage: High friction between billet and chamber walls. High force required. High scrap (butt). • Backward (Indirect) Extrusion: Die is mounted on a hollow ram. Ram pushes into the billet. Metal flows in the opposite direction to the ram movement through the hollow ram. Sketch: Show ram pushing right, metal exiting left (backwards) through the hollow ram. Advantage: Zero wall friction. 25-30% less force required than forward extrusion. Disadvantage: Mechanically complex tooling, hollow ram limits the maximum load that can be applied.

**14. Wire vs Tube Drawing (4-7 Marks):** Tier 1. • Wire Drawing: Pulling a solid metal rod through a converging die to reduce its diameter. Force is applied as tension at the exit. • Tube Drawing: Reducing the diameter and wall thickness of a hollow tube. • Key Difference for Full Marks: Tube drawing requires a mandrel inside the tube to support the internal walls and control the final internal diameter. Wire drawing has no internal support. • Diagrams to Draw: 1. Wire drawing die (solid metal passing through). 2. Tube drawing setup showing the die AND the Mandrel. • Types of Mandrels (Mention for 7M): Fixed mandrel, floating mandrel, moving mandrel.

### 🤖 Antigravity
**GTU MID-SEM: MISSING THEORY ANSWERS**

---

**1. THE ROLLING JUSTIFICATION (From Q2b - 3-4 Marks):** Question: "Rolling process gives better performance for poor/zero lubrication." Justify. GTU Marking Scheme Answer: • The Core Reason (1 Mark): Unlike other forming processes where friction is an enemy, in rolling, friction is an absolute necessity. It is the driving force that physically pulls the metal into the roll gap. • The Technical Condition (1 Mark): For the plate to be drawn into the rolls, the coefficient of friction (mu) must be greater than or equal to the tangent of the bite angle (alpha). Formula: mu >= tan(alpha). • The Justification (1-2 Marks): If lubrication is "perfect" (meaning zero friction), mu becomes 0. The rolls will simply spin and slip against the surface of the plate without gripping it. Therefore, "poor" or "zero" lubrication (which means HIGH friction) is actually required for the rolls to grip the metal and perform the rolling action.

---

**2. TIG VS MIG WELDING (From Q3a - 3 Marks):** Question: Differentiate between TIG and MIG welding processes. GTU Marking Scheme Answer (Write any 3-4 points for 3 Marks): • ELECTRODE: TIG: Uses a Non-consumable Tungsten electrode. MIG: Uses a Consumable metal wire electrode. • FILLER METAL: TIG: Must be fed separately by hand (if required). MIG: The electrode itself melts and acts as the filler. • WELDING SPEED: TIG: Slower (requires two hands, one for torch, one for filler). MIG: Faster (continuous automatic wire feed). • SHEET THICKNESS: TIG: Best for thin sheets and precision work (aerospace). MIG: Best for thicker plates and heavy fabrication. • SPATTER: TIG: Almost zero spatter, very clean weld. MIG: Produces some spatter.

---

**3. PURPOSE OF ELECTRODE COATING IN DETAIL (From Q3b - 7 Marks):** Question: Explain the purposes of coating provided on the coated electrode in detail. GTU Marking Scheme Answer (Write 6-7 points for 7 Marks): 1. Gaseous Shielding: The coating burns and decomposes to produce protective gases (like CO2 and CO). This gas shield displaces atmospheric oxygen and nitrogen, preventing the weld from becoming porous and brittle. 2. Slag Formation: The coating melts to form a blanket of liquid slag over the weld pool. This protects the solidifying metal from oxidation and slows down the cooling rate, preventing a brittle joint. 3. Arc Stabilization: The coating contains chemical compounds (like potassium and sodium silicates) that ionize easily in the heat. This helps to establish and maintain a steady, continuous arc. 4. Deoxidation: The coating contains deoxidizers (like ferromanganese or ferrosilicon) that actively react with and remove oxygen/impurities already inside the molten weld pool. 5. Alloying Additions: Specific alloying elements (like Chromium, Nickel, or Molybdenum) are mixed into the coating to transfer into the weld pool, improving the mechanical strength and corrosion resistance of the final joint. 6. Positional Welding Support: The freezing slag acts as a physical mold or "dam" that holds the liquid metal in place, allowing the welder to weld in vertical and overhead positions without the metal dripping out. 7. Spatter Reduction: The coating forms a slight cup at the tip of the electrode as it burns, which helps direct the arc precisely and reduces metal spatter.

---

Question: Explain various casting defects with causes and remedies.

Answer (Write 5-6 for 7M):

BLOW HOLES Cause: Moisture in sand, low permeability, excessive gas generation. Remedy: Use dry sand. Improve venting. Increase permeability of moulding sand.

MISRUN (Incomplete filling) Cause: Pouring temperature too low, thin sections in casting, improper gating. Remedy: Increase pouring temperature. Redesign gating system to ensure proper flow.

SHRINKAGE CAVITY Cause: Insufficient molten metal to feed the casting during solidification. No riser or riser is too small. Remedy: Use adequately sized risers. Place risers at the last point to solidify (hot spot).

HOT TEARS (Hot Cracks) Cause: The casting cannot shrink freely because the mould or core is too rigid (poor collapsibility). Remedy: Improve collapsibility of sand. Add proper fillets at corners. Avoid sharp changes in section thickness.

COLD SHUT Cause: Two streams of molten metal meet but do not fuse because they have cooled too much. Remedy: Increase pouring temperature. Pour faster. Redesign gating to avoid divided flow.

METAL PENETRATION Cause: Sand grains are too coarse. Molten metal seeps between sand particles. Remedy: Use finer sand. Apply mould wash/coating on the cavity surface.

WARPAGE / DISTORTION Cause: Uneven cooling of different sections of the casting. Remedy: Design uniform wall thickness. Use distortion allowance in pattern.

GATING SYSTEM ELEMENTS (3-4 Marks) Asked: 5/9 papers (Borderline)
Question: Explain the elements of a gating system with a sketch.

Answer:

A gating system is the network of channels that delivers molten metal from the ladle to the mould cavity.

ELEMENTS (in order of metal flow):

POURING BASIN: A funnel-shaped cup at the top where molten metal is first poured. Reduces turbulence and prevents slag from entering.
SPRUE: A vertical tapered channel that carries metal downward from the pouring basin. Tapered (narrow at bottom) to prevent air aspiration.
SPRUE WELL: A small reservoir at the base of the sprue. Absorbs the kinetic energy of falling metal and reduces turbulence.
RUNNER: A horizontal channel at the parting line that distributes metal from the sprue to the gates.
GATE (INGATE): The final opening through which metal enters the mould cavity. Controls the flow rate and direction of metal entry.
RISER: A reservoir of molten metal connected to the casting. Feeds extra metal to compensate for shrinkage during solidification. Last to solidify.
Diagram: Draw a cross-section of a mould showing all 6 elements connected in sequence from top to bottom. Label each clearly.

### 🧑 You
<USER_REQUEST>
Question: Explain the properties of moulding sand.

Answer (Write 6-7 properties for 7M, any 4 for 4M):

Permeability: The ability of moulding sand to allow gases and steam to pass through it during pouring. If permeability is low, gases get trapped and cause blow holes in the casting.

Flowability (Plasticity): The ability of sand to flow around the pattern and take the exact shape of the mould cavity. Better flowability = better reproduction of fine details.

Collapsibility: The ability of the mould to collapse (break down) easily after the casting solidifies. If sand does not collapse, the casting cannot shrink freely and internal stresses or cracks develop.

Cohesiveness (Strength): The ability of sand grains to stick together. Without cohesiveness, the mould walls will crumble when molten metal is poured.

Refractoriness: The ability of sand to withstand high temperatures of molten metal without fusing or melting. Silica sand has high refractoriness (~1700 deg C).

Green Strength: The strength of the mould in its wet (green) condition before drying. Must be enough to hold its shape during handling and core setting.

Adhesiveness: The ability of sand to stick to the walls of the moulding flask. Without this, the sand falls away from the flask during mould handling.

WELDING DEFECTS — CAUSES AND REMEDIES (4-7 Marks) Asked: 3/9 papers
Question: Explain various welding defects with their causes and remedies.

Answer (Write 5-6 defects for 7M):

POROSITY (Gas Holes) Cause: Gases trapped in the weld pool due to moisture, grease, or contaminated electrode. Remedy: Clean the base metal properly. Use dry electrodes. Ensure proper shielding gas flow.

UNDERCUT Cause: Excessive welding current or too high arc length. A groove forms along the weld toe. Remedy: Reduce current. Use correct arc length. Use proper weaving technique.

INCOMPLETE PENETRATION Cause: Low welding current, too fast travel speed, or too large root gap. Remedy: Increase current. Reduce travel speed. Use proper joint design with correct root gap.

SLAG INCLUSION Cause: Slag from the previous pass is not cleaned before the next pass is deposited. Remedy: Thoroughly chip and wire-brush slag between every pass.

SPATTER Cause: Excessive current, long arc, or wrong polarity. Remedy: Reduce current. Shorten arc length. Use correct polarity for the electrode type.

CRACK (Hot or Cold) Cause: Hot cracks — high sulphur/phosphorus in base metal. Cold cracks — hydrogen embrittlement, rapid cooling. Remedy: Use low-hydrogen electrodes. Pre-heat the base metal. Control cooling rate.

LACK OF FUSION Cause: Low heat input, wrong electrode angle, or oxide layer on the surface preventing fusion. Remedy: Increase current. Maintain proper electrode angle. Clean the joint surface.

fist chck did i do it above these texgt or not then do it for this text ; Question: Discuss various types of rolling mill arrangements.

Answer (Draw neat sketch for each):

TWO-HIGH MILL (Non-Reversing) Two rolls rotating in one fixed direction. Workpiece passes in one direction only. Must be returned to the entry side for next pass. Slow.

TWO-HIGH REVERSING MILL Same as above but roll direction can be reversed. Work passes back and forth. Eliminates return time. Used for blooming/slabbing.

THREE-HIGH MILL Three rolls stacked vertically. Metal passes forward between bottom and middle rolls, then backward between middle and top rolls. No reversing needed. Faster than two-high.

FOUR-HIGH MILL Two small-diameter work rolls (in contact with metal) backed by two large-diameter backup rolls. Small work rolls reduce the required rolling force. Used for rolling thin sheets and strips.

CLUSTER / SENDZIMIR MILL Each small work roll is backed by TWO or more backup rolls. Used for very thin foil rolling (aluminum foil, etc.). Very high rigidity.

TANDEM / CONTINUOUS MILL Multiple two-high or four-high stands arranged in a line. The workpiece passes through all stands in one continuous pass. Each stand reduces thickness progressively. Used for high-volume production (steel strips).

INGOT, BLOOM, BILLET, SLAB DEFINITIONS (3 Marks) Asked: 4/9 papers (Borderline)
Question: Define ingot, bloom, billet, and slab.

Answer:

INGOT: The first solid form of metal cast from molten metal in a mould. It is a large, heavy block and is the starting material for ALL rolling operations.

BLOOM: When an ingot is rolled, the first product is a bloom. Cross-section is SQUARE, typically larger than 150mm x 150mm.

BILLET: A bloom is further rolled to produce a billet. Cross-section is also SQUARE but smaller than 150mm x 150mm. Billets are used to make rods, bars, and wire.

SLAB: When an ingot is rolled into a RECTANGULAR cross-section (width much greater than thickness), it is called a slab. Slabs are used to make plates, sheets, and strips.

THE CHAIN: Ingot -> Bloom (square, large) -> Billet (square, small) -> Rods/Bars/Wire Ingot -> Slab (rectangular) -> Plates/Sheets/Strips

** Q2 State the objectives of a good plant layout 3 Marks ** The objectives of a good plant layout are 1 ** Minimum material handling ** Reduce the distance and frequency of material movement to decrease cost and time 2 ** Effective utilization of floor space ** Make optimum use of available area in all three dimensions length breadth height 3 ** Smooth and continuous flow of work ** Avoid backtracking and cross movement of materials 4 ** Worker comfort and safety ** Provide proper lighting ventilation temperature and safety measures to reduce accidents and fatigue 5 ** Flexibility ** The layout should be easily adaptable to future changes in product design volume or process 6 ** Minimum investment in equipment ** Reduce duplication of machines and tools 7 ** Better supervision and control ** Easy visual control and monitoring of production activities 8 ** Maximum utilization of machines and labour ** Reduce idle time of men and machines ---
** Q3 Explain Fixed Position Layout 3 Marks ** ** Definition ** In fixed position layout the major product or component remains stationary at one fixed location All the workers machines tools materials and equipment are brought to the product ** Characteristics ** The product is too heavy bulky or fragile to move Highly skilled labour is required Material handling is minimum for the product but maximum for tools and workers Production volume is very low one or few units at a time ** Advantages ** 1 Minimum movement of the product reduces damage 2 Continuity of operations is not affected by breakdown of any one machine 3 Greater flexibility layout can be easily changed 4 Personal job satisfaction worker completes the whole product ** Disadvantages ** 1 Requires highly skilled workers 2 Movement of workers tools and materials is high increases cost 3 Scheduling and coordination is complex 4 Large work area is needed ** Examples ** Ship building dam construction bridge building aircraft manufacturing boiler manufacturing ---

**Q6 What is Productivity Define various ways to increase/improve Productivity 7 Marks****Definition of Productivity**Productivity is the ratio of output produced to the input resources used in the production process**Productivity Output / Input**Where Output Goods produced or services delivered in quantity or value Input Resources used labour materials capital energy time**Types of Productivity****Labour Productivity**Output / Labour hours**Material Productivity**Output / Material consumed**Machine Productivity**Output / Machine hours**Capital Productivity**Output / Capital invested**Total Factor Productivity**Total output / Total input all factors combined**Ways to Increase/Improve Productivity****1 Improved Technology**Use modern machines automation CNC machines robotics to produce more output per unit time**2 Better Methods and Processes**Apply method study and work study techniques to eliminate wasteful operations and simplify processes**3 Training and Skill Development**Train workers in efficient methods multi skilling and quality awareness to increase their output**4 Material Management**Proper inventory control reduce material wastage use better quality raw materials**5 Better Plant Layout**Efficient layout reduces material handling backtracking and idle time**6 Incentive Schemes**Wage incentive plans motivate workers to produce more piece rate bonus schemes**7 Quality Control**Reduce defects and rework by implementing quality at source TQM and SQC**8 Maintenance Management**Preventive and predictive maintenance reduces machine breakdown and downtime**9 Working Conditions**Proper lighting ventilation temperature safety measures reduce fatigue and accidents**10 Research and Development**Develop new products better designs and improved processes ---

**Q11 What is a method study List the objectives of the method study 3 4 Marks****Definition**Method study is the systematic recording and critical examination of existing and proposed ways of doing work as a means of developing and applying easier and more effective methods and reducing costs**Objectives of Method Study**1 To improve the processes and procedures of production 2 To improve the layout of the factory workshop and workplace 3 To improve the design of plant and equipment 4 To reduce human effort and fatigue 5 To improve the use of materials machines and manpower 6 To develop a better physical working environment 7 To reduce unnecessary material handling 8 To standardize the best method of doing work 9 To reduce the cost of production by eliminating wasteful and unnecessary operations ---

**Q12 Write the basic procedure of the method study 3 Marks**The basic procedure of method study consists of the following steps 1**SELECT**Select the work or process to be studied Priority is given to jobs with high cost high labour content bottleneck operations or high rejection rate 2**RECORD**Record all the relevant facts about the existing method using process charts flow diagrams and other recording techniques 3**EXAMINE**Critically examine the recorded facts by questioning every detail What is done Why Where When Who How Purpose Place Sequence Person Means 4**DEVELOP**Develop the most practical economic and effective method by considering all alternatives Apply principles of motion economy 5**EVALUATE**Evaluate the new method considering cost effectiveness time savings and feasibility 6**DEFINE**Define the new method clearly in writing so that it can be easily understood and followed 7**INSTALL**Install the new method as standard practice Train workers in the new method 8**MAINTAIN**Maintain the new method by regular checking to ensure workers follow it correctly and no unauthorized changes are made**Memory Aid S R E D E D I M Select Record Examine Develop Evaluate Define Install Maintain**---

**Q15 Explain the two handed process chart with a neat sketch 4 7 Marks****Definition**A Two Handed Process Chart also called Left Hand Right Hand Chart or Operator Process Chart is a process chart that records the activities of the left hand and right hand of an operator in relation to each other It is used for studying repetitive short cycle operations performed at a single workplace**Symbols Used**Symbol Meaning --- --- --- --- --- Operation hand grasps positions uses or releases a tool/object Transport hand moves to or from the work D Delay hand waits idle Hold hand holds the work piece no movement**How to Prepare**1 Observe the operation several times 2 Record what the left hand does and what the right hand does simultaneously 3 Each activity is recorded side by side left column for left hand right column for right hand 4 Activities happening at the same time are written on the same row**Example Two Handed Process Chart for Assembling Nut and Bolt**Left Hand LH Symbol LH Symbol RH Right Hand RH --- --- --- --- --- --- --- --- --- --- --- --- --- --- --- --- Reach to bolt bin Reach to nut bin Grasp bolt Grasp nut Carry bolt to assembly area Carry nut to assembly area Hold bolt upright Position nut on bolt Hold bolt Tighten nut turn clockwise Release assembled piece Release assembled piece Move to finished bin Move to finished bin Drop assembled piece D Wait idle**Summary**Activity Left Hand Right Hand --- --- --- --- --- --- --- --- --- Operations 3 4 Transport 3 3 Delay D 0 1 Hold 2 0**Total****8****8****Advantages**1 Identifies idle time of either hand helps balance workload 2 Helps redesign the workplace to reduce unnecessary movements 3 Useful for training new workers in the correct method 4 Helps apply principles of motion economy See Diagram Prompt section at end for visual ---

**1 Burns Classification Pathophysiology and Comprehensive Management** **Definition** A burn is an acute traumatic injury to the skin subcutaneous tissues or deeper structures caused by thermal energy flame scald contact chemical agents electricity or radiation resulting in protein denaturation cellular necrosis and systemic burn shock **Classification by Depth of Injury** **1st Degree Superficial** • Anatomy Epidermis only • Clinical Appearance Erythematous dry no blisters • Sensation Hyperesthetic painful • Healing / Intervention Heals in 3 6 days no scarring **2nd Degree Superficial Partial** • Anatomy Epidermis and Papillary Dermis • Clinical Appearance Pink / red moist thin walled bullae brisk refill • Sensation Intensely painful • Healing / Intervention Heals in 7 14 days minimal scar **2nd Degree Deep Partial** • Anatomy Epidermis and Reticular Dermis • Clinical Appearance Mottled white / red waxy dry sluggish refill • Sensation Dull pinprick sensation • Healing / Intervention Heals in 3 8 weeks with hypertrophic scar needs excision and grafting **3rd Degree Full Thickness** • Anatomy Entire Epidermis and Dermis • Clinical Appearance Leathery porcelain white / charred non blanching • Sensation Insensate anesthetic • Healing / Intervention Requires surgical excision and skin grafting **4th Degree Subdermal** • Anatomy Extends into fat fascia muscle bone • Clinical Appearance Charred blackened exposed bone / muscle • Sensation Anesthetic • Healing / Intervention Debridement flap coverage or amputation **Pathophysiology of Burn Injury** • Jacksons Zones of Burn Injury • Zone of Coagulation Central Point of maximum damage irreversible protein denaturation and tissue necrosis • Zone of Stasis Middle Compromised tissue perfusion and ischemia salvageable with adequate fluid resuscitation prone to secondary necrosis if untreated • Zone of Hyperemia Outer Peripheral vasodilation due to inflammatory mediator release viable tissue that recovers spontaneously • System

CARDIAC SURGERIES Easy-language study notes | Based on the supplied Cardiac Surgeries / Bailey & Love pages 823–849 These notes simplify the supplied source for exam revision. Where the requested heading (especially contraindications, heart transplant, or physiotherapy) is not specifically detailed in the supplied pages, it is clearly treated as a general study addition rather than presented as source text. 1. OPEN-HEART SURGERY Introduction / Definition Open-heart surgery is an operation in which the chest is opened so the surgeon can directly operate on the heart or great vessels. The heart is commonly approached through a median sternotomy. Types • On-pump open-heart surgery – uses cardiopulmonary bypass (CPB). • Off-pump surgery – selected operations, such as some CABG procedures, are performed on the beating heart. Indications • CABG for suitable coronary artery disease. • Congenital heart defects requiring repair or palliation. • Aortic aneurysm/dissection surgery. • Selected valve and other cardiac operations. Contraindications / limitations The supplied chapter does not give a single list of absolute contraindications. In practice, surgery is limited when operative risk is greater than expected benefit, anatomy is unsuitable, or severe irreversible comorbidity makes surgery inappropriate. Procedure • Anaesthesia and monitoring. • Median sternotomy and opening of the pericardium. • Heparinisation if CPB is required. • CPB cannulation and establishment of bypass. • Myocardial protection and the planned repair. • Rewarming, restoration of coronary flow and weaning from CPB. • Reverse anticoagulation and close the chest. CARDIAC SURGERIES Easy-language study notes | Based on the supplied Cardiac Surgeries / Bailey & Love pages 823–849. These notes simplify the supplied source for exam revision. Where the requested heading (especially contraindications, heart transplant, or physiotherapy) is not specifically detailed in the supplied pages, it is clearly treated as a general study addition rather than presented as source text.

- **1. OPEN-HEART SURGERY**
  - **Introduction / Definition:** Open-heart surgery is an operation in which the chest is opened so the surgeon can directly operate on the heart or great vessels. The heart is commonly approached through a median sternotomy.
  - **Types:**
    - On-pump open-heart surgery: uses cardiopulmonary bypass (CPB).
    - Off-pump surgery: selected operations, such as some CABG procedures, are performed on the beating heart.
  - **Indications:**
    - CABG for suitable coronary artery disease.
    - Congenital heart defects requiring repair or palliation.
    - Aortic aneurysm/dissection surgery.
    - Selected valve and other cardiac operations.
  - **Contraindications / limitations:** The supplied chapter does not give a single list of absolute contraindications. In practice, surgery is limited when operative risk is greater than expected benefit, anatomy is unsuitable, or severe irreversible comorbidity makes surgery inappropriate.
  - **Procedure:**
    - Anaesthesia and monitoring.
    - Median sternotomy and opening of the pericardium.
    - Heparinisation if CPB is required.
    - CPB cannulation and establishment of bypass.
    - Myocardial protection and the planned repair.
    - Rewarming, restoration of coronary flow and weaning from CPB.
    - Reverse anticoagulation and close the chest.
    - The source describes median sternotomy, full heparinisation before CPB, and alternative arterial/venous cannulation sites. (filecite turn10file5 L334-L359)
  - **Complications:**
    - Bleeding/coagulopathy.
    - Arrhythmias and low cardiac output.
    - Respiratory complications.
 
<truncated 16910 bytes>
:**
    - Antiplatelet/statin/anti-anginal therapy when indicated after CABG.
    - Anticoagulation reversal after CPB with protamine as described in the source.
    - Inotropes may be required for low cardiac output.
    - Oxygen, fluids/electrolyte correction and rhythm treatment according to clinical need.
    - For CABG, the source describes treatment of postoperative low cardiac output through oxygenation, preload/afterload optimisation, rhythm management and improved contractility; pharmacological or mechanical support may be needed. (filecite turn12file4 L293-L313)
  - **Physiotherapy management:**
    - Positioning and frequent repositioning.
    - Diaphragmatic breathing and thoracic expansion exercises.
    - Supported cough/huff and secretion clearance when indicated.
    - Active ROM of limbs and ankle/foot exercises.
    - Early mobilisation: bed mobility → sitting → standing → walking.
    - Progressive walking and cardiac rehabilitation.
    - Monitor HR, BP, SpO2, RR, symptoms and exercise tolerance.
    - Stop/modify activity for new chest pain, marked breathlessness, dizziness/syncope, significant arrhythmia or haemodynamic instability.

- **10. QUICK EXAM REVISION**
  - **Open-heart surgery:** direct surgery on heart/great vessels, commonly through median sternotomy.
  - **CPB:** temporarily takes over circulation + ventilation.
  - **CPB flow:** venous blood → reservoir → oxygenator → pump → arterial system.
  - **Myocardial protection:** cardioplegia, cross-clamp fibrillation, total circulatory arrest.
  - **CABG:** bypasses coronary blockage with a graft; LIMA is important for LAD.
  - **Aneurysm:** abnormal aortic dilatation; rupture may cause haemorrhage/tamponade.
  - **Tamponade:** pericardial pressure compresses heart → reduced venous return/cardiac output; urgent drainage is required when unstable.
  - **CHD:** structural heart abnormality present from birth.
  - **Shunt:** abnormal blood pathway; left-to-right increases pulmonary flow, right-to-left may cause cyanosis.
