<USER_REQUEST>
Burns & Plastic Surgery — S. Das

BPT Exam Notes | Easy language, moderately explained

Note: These are simplified, exam-oriented notes based on standard surgical concepts covered in S. Das. They are paraphrased rather than copied verbatim.

⸻

1. BURNS

Definition

A burn is an injury to the skin and sometimes deeper tissues caused by heat, chemicals, electricity, radiation or friction.

Etiology

* Thermal: flame, hot liquid, steam, hot objects.
* Chemical: acids and alkalis.
* Electrical: electrical current.
* Radiation: sunlight, radiotherapy, etc.
* Friction: skin injury due to rubbing.

Indications for Hospital/Surgical Management

* Deep or extensive burns.
* Burns involving face, hands, feet, genitalia, major joints.
* Circumferential burns.
* Electrical or chemical burns.
* Inhalation injury.
* Burns requiring skin grafting.

Contraindications

There are no absolute contraindications to emergency burn treatment.
Definitive surgery may be delayed if the patient is haemodynamically unstable or medically unfit.

Risk Factors

* Children and elderly people.
* Occupational exposure to fire/chemicals.
* Smoking and unsafe cooking practices.
* Poor electrical safety.
* Lack of protective equipment.

Types

According to depth:

1. Superficial burn – epidermis involved; red and painful.
2. Partial-thickness burn – epidermis and part of dermis involved; blistering and pain.
3. Full-thickness burn – entire skin thickness destroyed; skin may be white, brown or charred and sensation may be reduced.
4. Deep burn – extends into deeper tissues such as fat, muscle or bone.

According to cause: thermal, chemical, electrical and radiation burns.

Investigation

* Estimate percentage of total body surface area (TBSA) burned.
* Assess burn depth.
* Vital signs and urine output.
* CBC, electrolytes, renal function.
* ABG and carboxyhaemoglobin when inhalation injury is suspected.
* ECG in electrical burns.
* X-ray/other imaging when associated injuries are suspected.

Management

Immediate managemen
<truncated 45615 bytes>
ment

Contraindications

Depend on the specific operation:
	•	Severe uncorrectable medical illness
	•	Active uncontrolled infection
	•	Patient unfit for anesthesia

Risk Factors
	•	Smoking
	•	Alcohol
	•	GERD
	•	Obesity
	•	Achalasia
	•	Previous esophageal injury

Types of Surgery
	1.	Heller myotomy – commonly for achalasia.
	2.	POEM – endoscopic muscle-cutting procedure for achalasia.
	3.	Esophageal dilatation – for strictures.
	4.	Esophagectomy – removal of part/all of the esophagus, usually for cancer.
	5.	Esophageal reconstruction – restoring food passage after esophageal removal.

Surgical Procedure – Heller Myotomy
	•	Small abdominal/chest incisions are made.
	•	The tight lower esophageal muscle is identified.
	•	Muscle fibers are divided.
	•	The mucosa is preserved.
	•	Often combined with an anti-reflux procedure.

Investigations
	•	Upper GI endoscopy
	•	Barium swallow
	•	Esophageal manometry
	•	CT scan
	•	Biopsy when malignancy is suspected

Management
	•	Treat underlying cause
	•	Dietary modification
	•	Endoscopic dilatation when appropriate
	•	Surgery for severe structural/motility disease
	•	Nutritional support
	•	Swallowing rehabilitation

Complications
	•	Esophageal perforation
	•	Bleeding
	•	Infection
	•	Aspiration
	•	GERD
	•	Anastomotic leak after major reconstruction
	•	Recurrent dysphagia

⸻

⭐ Quick Revision

Embolectomy → removal of embolus
Vascular reconstruction → repair/replacement/bypass of vessel
Aneurysm surgery → repair of dilated weak vessel
Amputation → removal of non-viable limb
Gangrene surgery → removal of dead/infected tissue
DVT → clot in deep vein
Pulmonary embolism → clot blocks pulmonary artery
Kidney transplant → replacement of failed kidney
Portal hypertension → increased portal venous pressure
Esophageal surgery → treatment of structural/motility causes of dysphagia

Absolutely. Here are Thoracic Surgeries in the same order, using simple, exam-oriented language.

THORACIC SURGERIES

1. Thoracoplasty

Definition

Thoracoplasty is a surgical procedure in which part of the ribs is removed to collapse the chest wall over a diseased part of the lung, helping the lung cavity to close.

Etiology / Indications
	•	Old pulmonary tuberculosis with persistent cavity
	•	Chronic empyema
	•	Bronchopleural fistula
	•	Persistent infected pleural cavity
	•	Destroyed lung when other procedures are unsuitable

Contraindications
	•	Severe respiratory insufficiency
	•	Extensive bilateral lung disease
	•	Patient unfit for major surgery
	•	Active uncontrolled infection

Risk Factors
	•	Chronic pulmonary TB
	•	Poor nutrition
	•	Chronic infection
	•	Smoking
	•	Previous thoracic surgery

Types
	1.	Complete thoracoplasty – extensive rib removal.
	2.	Partial thoracoplasty – only selected ribs are removed.
	3.	Apical thoracoplasty – mainly upper ribs are removed to collapse the apical cavity.

Surgical Procedure
	•	Incision is made over the chest wall.
	•	Selected ribs are exposed.
	•	Portions of ribs are removed.
	•	Chest wall collapses inward.
	•	The underlying cavity is compressed.

Investigations
	•	Chest X-ray
	•	CT chest
	•	Pulmonary function tests
	•	Sputum examination
	•	Blood investigations

Management
	•	Anti-tubercular treatment when indicated
	•	Antibiotics for infection
	•	Pain management
	•	Respiratory physiotherapy
	•	Breathing exercises
	•	Gradual mobilization

Complications
	•	Chest deformity
	•	Reduced chest expansion
	•	Restrictive respiratory impairment
	•	Pain
	•	Infection
	•	Bleeding
	•	Poor shoulder movement

⸻

2. Pulmonary Resection Surgery

Definition

Pulmonary resection is surgery in which a diseased portion of the lung is removed.

Etiology / Indications
	•	Lung cancer
	•	Bronchiectasis
	•	Lung abscess
	•	Localized tuberculosis
	•	Pulmonary cysts
	•	Localized destroyed 
<truncated 13754 bytes>
lignant pleural disease
	•	Chronic pleural infection in selected cases

Contraindications
	•	Severe respiratory/cardiac disease
	•	Patient unfit for surgery
	•	Extensive disease where surgery is not beneficial

Risk Factors
	•	Recurrent pneumothorax
	•	Smoking
	•	Pleural malignancy
	•	Chronic pleural infection
	•	Previous thoracic disease

Types
	1.	Partial pleurectomy – part of pleura is removed.
	2.	Total pleurectomy – extensive removal of pleura.
	3.	Pleurectomy/decortication – pleura and fibrous peel are removed in selected diseases.

Surgical Procedure
	•	Chest is accessed, often using VATS or open thoracotomy.
	•	Diseased pleura is identified.
	•	Pleura is carefully separated and removed.
	•	Chest drains are placed.
	•	Chest is closed.

Investigations
	•	Chest X-ray
	•	CT chest
	•	Pleural fluid analysis
	•	Pleural biopsy
	•	PFT
	•	Blood tests

Management
	•	Pain control
	•	Chest drainage
	•	Breathing exercises
	•	Incentive spirometry
	•	Supported coughing
	•	Early mobilization
	•	Treatment of underlying disease

Complications
	•	Bleeding
	•	Air leak
	•	Infection
	•	Persistent pain
	•	Respiratory complications
	•	Reduced lung expansion

⸻

⭐ Quick Revision
	1.	Thoracoplasty → removal of ribs to collapse chest wall/cavity
	2.	Pulmonary resection → removal of diseased lung tissue
	3.	Thoracotomy → surgical opening of chest
	4.	Lobectomy → removal of one lung lobe
	5.	Pneumonectomy → removal of entire lung
	6.	Decortication → removal of fibrous pleural peel
	7.	Pneumothorax surgery → treat persistent/recurrent air leak
	8.	Hydropneumothorax surgery → drain air + fluid and treat cause
	9.	Empyema surgery → drain pus ± decortication
	10.	TB surgery → remove/manage localized destroyed or complicated lung disease
	11.	Lung transplant → replacement of diseased lung(s)
	12.	Pleurectomy → removal of pleura

1. ARTHRODESIS Definition Arthrodesis is a surgical procedure in which a joint is deliberately fused by removing articular cartilage and stabilizing the joint until bony union occurs, permanently eliminating movement at that joint. Objectives • Relieve severe pain • Provide a stable joint • Correct deformity • Improve function • Prevent further joint destruction Indications • Severe osteoarthritis or rheumatoid arthritis • Post-traumatic arthritis • Joint destruction after trauma • Selected controlled infective or tuberculous arthritis • Severe joint instability • Paralytic instability • Congenital deformity • Failed reconstruction or joint replacement • When joint replacement is unsuitable Contraindications • Active uncontrolled infection • Poor vascularity • Severe osteoporosis • Poor general health preventing surgery • Inadequate soft-tissue coverage • Severe disease of adjacent joints Classification • By site: spinal, shoulder, elbow, wrist, hip, knee, ankle and small-joint arthrodesis • By technique: extra-articular, intra-articular and combined • By fixation: cast/immobilization, internal fixation, external fixation or combined Principles • Adequate exposure • Remove diseased cartilage • Prepare subchondral bone • Correct deformity • Position joint in a functional position • Obtain good bone-to-bone contact • Provide stable fixation• Use bone graft when required • Preserve blood supply • Immobilize until solid union General surgical procedure • Expose joint • Remove cartilage and diseased tissue • Freshen bone surfaces • Correct deformity • Position surfaces in functional alignment • Insert bone graft when indicated • Fix with screws, plates, nails or other fixation • Close wound and immobilize Postoperative & physiotherapy management • Pain and edema control • Wound care and radiographic monitoring • Respiratory/circulatory exercises when indicated • Active movement of uninvolved joints • Isometric ex
<truncated 13043 bytes>
vs incomplete amputation • By level: finger, hand, wrist, forearm, elbow, arm; toe, foot, ankle, leg, knee, thigh • By mechanism: clean-cut, crush, avulsion and combined crush-avulsion Principles • Resuscitate and control bleeding • Preserve amputated part correctly • Debride nonviable tissue • Prepare/shorten bone when required • Restore skeletal stability• Repair tendons • Repair arteries and veins • Repair nerves • Provide soft-tissue coverage • Monitor circulation closely Preservation of amputated part • Wrap in sterile moist gauze • Place in a clean waterproof bag • Place the bag in an ice/cold-water mixture • Do not place the amputated part directly on ice or directly in water Surgical sequence • Debridement • Bone preparation and fixation • Tendon repair • Arterial microsurgical anastomosis • Venous repair • Nerve repair • Skin/soft-tissue coverage • Postoperative vascular monitoring Physiotherapy management • Protect repairs and control edema • Maintain safe-joint mobility • Respiratory/circulatory exercises • Appropriate isometrics • Progressive ROM after healing • Tendon-gliding where appropriate • Strengthening • Sensory re-education • Scar and edema management • Fine-motor training for upper limb • Gait training for lower limb • Proprioception and functional/return-to-work training Complications • Vascular thrombosis • Arterial occlusion • Venous congestion • Infection • Compartment syndrome • Tissue necrosis• Joint stiffness • Muscle weakness • Tendon adhesions • Non-union/malunion • Cold intolerance • Neuroma • Poor sensory/motor recovery • Chronic pain • Functional limitation Conclusion Limb replantation is a complex multidisciplinary microsurgical procedure. **1 ARTHRODESIS** **Definition** Arthrodesis is a surgical procedure in which a joint is deliberately fused by removing articular cartilage and stabilizing the joint until bony union occurs permanently eliminating movement at that joint **Objectives** • Relieve severe pain • Provide a stable joint • Correct deformity • Improve function • Prevent further joint destruction **Indications** • Severe osteoarthritis or rheumatoid arthritis • Post traumatic arthritis • Joint destruction after trauma • Selected controlled infective or tuberculous arthritis • Severe joint instability • Paralytic instability • Congenital deformity • Failed reconstruction or joint replacement • When joint replacement is unsuitable **Contraindications** • Active uncontrolled infection • Poor vascularity • Severe osteoporosis • Poor general health preventing surgery • Inadequate soft tissue coverage • Severe disease of adjacent joints **Classification** • By site spinal shoulder elbow wrist hip knee ankle and small joint arthrodesis • By technique extra articular intra articular and combined • By fixation cast/immobilization internal fixation external fixation or combined **Principles** • Adequate exposure • Remove diseased cartilage • Prepare subchondral bone • Correct deformity • Position joint in a functional position • Obtain good bone to bone contact • Provide stable fixation • Use bone graft when required • Preserve blood supply • Immobilize until solid union **General surgical procedure** • Expose joint • Remove cartilage and diseased tissue • Freshen bone surfaces • Correct deformity • Position surfaces in functional alignment • Insert bone graft when indicated • Fix with screws plates nails or other fixation • Close wound and immobilize **Postoperative and physiotherapy management** • Pain and edema control • Wound care and radiographic monitoring • Respiratory/circulatory exercises when indicated • Active movement of uninvolved joints • Isome
<truncated 13081 bytes>
ciated injuries • Unsuitable amputated part • Severe medical instability **Classification** • Complete vs incomplete amputation • By level finger hand wrist forearm elbow arm toe foot ankle leg knee thigh • By mechanism clean cut crush avulsion and combined crush avulsion **Principles** • Resuscitate and control bleeding • Preserve amputated part correctly • Debride nonviable tissue • Prepare/shorten bone when required • Restore skeletal stability • Repair tendons • Repair arteries and veins • Repair nerves • Provide soft tissue coverage • Monitor circulation closely **Preservation of amputated part** • Wrap in sterile moist gauze • Place in a clean waterproof bag • Place the bag in an ice/cold water mixture • Do not place the amputated part directly on ice or directly in water **Surgical sequence** • Debridement • Bone preparation and fixation • Tendon repair • Arterial microsurgical anastomosis • Venous repair • Nerve repair • Skin/soft tissue coverage • Postoperative vascular monitoring **Physiotherapy management** • Protect repairs and control edema • Maintain safe joint mobility • Respiratory/circulatory exercises • Appropriate isometrics • Progressive ROM after healing • Tendon gliding where appropriate • Strengthening • Sensory re education • Scar and edema management • Fine motor training for upper limb • Gait training for lower limb • Proprioception and functional/return to work training **Complications** • Vascular thrombosis • Arterial occlusion • Venous congestion • Infection • Compartment syndrome • Tissue necrosis • Joint stiffness • Muscle weakness • Tendon adhesions • Non union/malunion • Cold intolerance • Neuroma • Poor sensory/motor recovery • Chronic pain • Functional limitation **Conclusion** Limb replantation is a complex multidisciplinary microsurgical procedure Outcome depends on injury level and mechanism ischaemia time tissue viability quality of repair and prolonged rehabilitation

1. Prolapsed Intervertebral Disc (PID)
Introduction & Definition
Definition: Prolapsed Intervertebral Disc (PID) is defined as the herniation or extrusion of the nucleus pulposus through a rent or tear in the surrounding annulus fibrosus, leading to compression of the adjacent neural structures (spinal nerve roots or spinal cord). It most commonly occurs in the lower lumbar spine (L4-L5 and L5-S1) due to high mechanical stress, but can also occur in the cervical spine (C5-C6, C6-C7).

Aetiology / Causes
Age: Most common in the 3rd and 4th decades of life when the nucleus is still gelatinous but the annulus has begun to weaken.
Trauma: A sudden lifting strain, twisting motion, or fall.
Degeneration: Age-related desiccation (drying out) and weakening of the annulus fibrosus.
Occupational hazards: Heavy manual labor, repetitive bending, or prolonged driving.
Pathophysiology & Stages of Herniation
The disc undergoes degenerative changes. A sudden mechanical load causes the semi-liquid nucleus pulposus to push through weakened annular fibers. Stages of PID:

Bulging Disc: The annulus remains intact but bulges outward.
Protrusion: The nucleus pushes into the outer layers of the annulus but is still contained.
Extrusion: The annulus ruptures completely, and the nucleus material escapes into the epidural space but remains connected to the central disc.
Sequestration: The extruded nuclear material breaks off entirely and becomes a free fragment in the spinal canal.
Clinical Features
Symptoms:

Low Back Pain: Usually the first symptom, sudden in onset, described as a "catch" in the back.
Sciatica (Radicular Pain): Pain radiating down the buttock, posterior thigh, calf, and into the foot, corresponding to the compressed nerve root's dermatome.
Neurological Symptoms: Tingling, numbness (paraesthesia), or weakness in the leg/foot.
Aggravating Factors: Pain worsens with coughing, sneezing, or straining (increases intraspinal pressure).
Signs (On Examination):

Posture: Loss of lumbar lor
<truncated 28709 bytes>
 Features
Spinal Deformity: The parents usually notice a lump on the back, uneven shoulders, or a visible curve in the spine (scoliosis) in infancy or early childhood.
Progression: The deformity worsens rapidly during periods of growth spurts.
Neurological symptoms: Usually absent initially. Severe, neglected curves can eventually cause spinal cord tethering or compression.
Signs: Asymmetrical trunk, rib hump on forward bending test (Adams test).
Investigations
X-rays (AP and Lateral of whole spine): Diagnostic. Shows the wedge-shaped anomalous bone and the degree of scoliosis (measured by Cobb's angle).
MRI of the Spine: Mandatory to rule out associated neural axis abnormalities like tethered cord, syringomyelia, or diastematomyelia, which are common with congenital spine defects.
Ultrasound (Renal and Cardiac): To rule out associated VACTERL anomalies.
Management
Because congenital curves are rigid and do not respond to bracing, management is largely observational or surgical.

A. Conservative Management (Observation)
Indicated for non-segmented or incarcerated hemivertebrae that are not progressing.
Serial X-rays every 6 months to monitor for curve progression. (Braces are ineffective for congenital scoliosis).
B. Surgical Management
Indications: Documented progression of the curve, fully segmented hemivertebrae in young children, or severe curves at presentation.

Hemivertebra Excision: The anomalous wedge of bone is surgically completely removed (via an anterior, posterior, or combined approach).
Short Segment Spinal Fusion: After excision, the spine above and below the defect is brought together, straightened, and fused using pedicle screws and rods. This corrects the deformity and prevents further asymmetric growth.
In Situ Fusion: Fusing the spine as it is (without excising the hemivertebra) in very young children to arrest the growth and stop the progression of the curve.
</USER_REQUEST>

PART I — SHOULDER Regional Conditions of the Shoulder — 20-Mark University Exam Answer Introduction The shoulder is a highly mobile joint complex consisting mainly of the glenohumeral, acromioclavicular, sternoclavicular and scapulothoracic articulations. Because of its wide range of movement and dependence on soft tissues for stability, it is commonly affected by painful disorders involving the joint capsule, rotator cuff tendons, biceps tendon and subacromial bursa. The important regional shoulder conditions include: 1. Periarthritis shoulder / Adhesive capsulitis 2. Rotator cuff tendinitis 3. Supraspinatus tendinitis 4. Infraspinatus tendinitis 5. Bicipital tendinitis 6. Subacromial bursitis 1. Periarthritis Shoulder / Adhesive Capsulitis Definition Adhesive capsulitis is a painful condition of the shoulder characterized by progressive pain, stiffness and marked restriction of both active and passive glenohumeral movements, particularly external rotation. It is commonly called frozen shoulder. Clinical Features • Gradual onset of shoulder pain. • Pain is often worse at night and may disturb sleep. • Progressive stiffness of the shoulder. • Difficulty with combing hair, dressing, reaching overhead and reaching behind the back. • Both active and passive ROM are restricted. • External rotation is usually the most restricted movement, followed by abduction and internal rotation. • Capsular pattern: External rotation > abduction > internal rotation. • Muscle weakness may develop secondary to pain and disuse. Page 1 BPT University Exam Notes• Scapular movements may become excessive as compensation. Stages 1. Freezing stage: increasing pain and progressive loss of movement. 2. Frozen stage: pain may decrease but marked stiffness persists. 3. Thawing stage: gradual recovery of ROM and function. Management Medical management • Patient education and activity modification. • Analgesics and NSAIDs as prescribed. • Corticosteroid injection, particularly in the painful early stage.
<truncated 33649 bytes>
orefoot pain. • Pain worsens with walking/running. Page 28 BPT University Exam Notes• Feeling of walking on a pebble may occur. • Callus formation. • Tenderness. • Reduced tolerance for prolonged standing. Management • Activity modification. • Proper footwear. • Avoid high heels. • Metatarsal pads. • Orthotics when indicated. • Ice. • Calf and foot stretching. • Foot intrinsic strengthening. • Gait correction. • Gradual return to activity. • Treat underlying foot deformity. 27. Morton's Neuroma Definition Morton's neuroma is a painful perineural fibrotic enlargement/irritation of a common digital nerve, most commonly occurring between the third and fourth toes. Causes/Risk Factors • Narrow footwear. • High heels. • Repetitive forefoot loading. • Running. • Foot deformities. Clinical Features • Burning forefoot pain. • Pain radiating toward toes. • Numbness/tingling. • Feeling of a pebble or lump inside the shoe. • Symptoms worsen with tight shoes. • Pain increases during walking/running. • Tenderness in interdigital space. • Clicking may be produced by compression of the forefoot (Mulder sign). Page 29 BPT University Exam NotesInvestigations • Clinical examination. • Ultrasound. • MRI in selected cases. Management • Wide toe-box footwear. • Avoid high heels. • Metatarsal pads. • Activity modification. • Orthotics where indicated. • Analgesics. • Foot intrinsic strengthening. • Calf stretching. • Foot mobility. • Gait correction. • Injection may be considered. • Surgical decompression/neurectomy in persistent cases.

tional training • Gradual return to running / jumping --- **26 Metatarsalgia** **Definition** Metatarsalgia is pain arising from the forefoot / metatarsal head region usually caused by excessive loading of the metatarsal heads **Causes** • Excessive running / jumping • Improper footwear • High heels • Pes cavus • Hallux valgus • Altered foot biomechanics • Increased body weight • Fat pad changes **Clinical Features** • Pain under metatarsal heads • Burning or aching forefoot pain • Pain worsens with walking / running • Feeling of walking on a pebble may occur • Callus formation • Tenderness • Reduced tolerance for prolonged standing **Management** • Activity modification • Proper footwear • Avoid high heels • Metatarsal pads • Orthotics when indicated • Ice • Calf and foot stretching • Foot intrinsic strengthening • Gait correction • Gradual return to activity • Treat underlying foot deformity --- **27 Mortons Neuroma** **Definition** Mortons neuroma is a painful perineural fibrotic enlargement / irritation of a common digital nerve most commonly occurring between the third and fourth toes **Causes / Risk Factors** • Narrow footwear • High heels • Repetitive forefoot loading • Running • Foot deformities **Clinical Features** • Burning forefoot pain • Pain radiating toward toes • Numbness / tingling • Feeling of a pebble or lump inside the shoe • Symptoms worsen with tight shoes • Pain increases during walking / running • Tenderness in interdigital space • Clicking may be produced by compression of the forefoot Mulder sign **Investigations** • Clinical examination • Ultrasound • MRI in selected cases **Management** • Wide toe box footwear • Avoid high heels • Metatarsal pads • Activity modification • Orthotics where indicated • Analgesics • Foot intrinsic strengthening • Calf stretching • Foot mobility • Gait correction • Injection may be considered • Surgical decompression / neurectomy in persistent cases

1. Congenital Talipes Equinovarus (CTEV / Clubfoot)
Introduction & Definition
CTEV is a complex congenital deformity of the foot characterized by four distinct components: Equinus (plantarflexion at the ankle), Varus (inversion of the subtalar joint), Adduction (of the forefoot), and Cavus (exaggerated medial longitudinal arch). It is commonly remembered by the mnemonic "CAVE".

Clinical Features
The foot is turned downwards and inwards, resembling a club.
Deep creases are visible on the medial border and posterior aspect of the heel.
The heel is small and empty (calcaneus is pulled up).
The calf muscles (especially on the affected side) are visibly atrophied and underdeveloped.
It is a rigid deformity that cannot be passively corrected to a neutral position.
Can be unilateral or bilateral.
Investigations
Clinical Diagnosis: Diagnosis is primarily clinical immediately at birth.
X-rays (AP and Lateral views of the foot in stress dorsiflexion): Used to assess the severity and document the baseline bony alignment (Kite's angle, talocalcaneal angle). X-rays are also used to monitor the progress of correction.
Complications
If untreated, the child walks on the dorsolateral aspect of the foot.
Formation of painful, large callosities and bursa over the weight-bearing area.
Awkward, limping gait.
"Rocker-bottom foot" (iatrogenic complication if the midfoot is forced into dorsiflexion before the heel equinus is corrected).
Severe psychological and cosmetic impact.
Medical / Conservative Management
Ponseti Method (Gold Standard): Serial manipulation and casting starting within the first week of life. The deformities are corrected in a specific order: Cavus, Adductus, Varus, and finally Equinus. Casts are changed weekly.
Splinting: After full correction, the foot is maintained in a Denis Browne splint or Foot Abduction Brace (full-time for 3 months, then at night until 4 years of age) to prevent relapse.
Surgical Management
Percutaneous Tendoachilles Tenotomy: Often required as the fin
<truncated 22067 bytes>
drome (TOS): If symptomatic, it causes compression of the brachial plexus (C8-T1 nerve roots) and the subclavian artery/vein.
Neurological: Pain, tingling, and numbness along the ulnar border of the forearm and hand. Weakness, clumsiness, and wasting of the intrinsic muscles of the hand (thenar and hypothenar eminences).
Vascular: The hand becomes cold, pale, and pulseless, especially when the arm is raised.
Adson's Test: Positive (radial pulse disappears upon taking a deep breath and turning the head toward the affected side).
Investigations
X-rays (AP Neck/Chest): Identifies the presence of a bony cervical rib or an elongated C7 transverse process.
Nerve Conduction Studies (EMG/NCV): Confirms neurological compression at the thoracic outlet (differentiating it from ulnar neuropathy at the elbow).
Doppler Ultrasound / Angiography: Used if vascular compression or a subclavian artery aneurysm is suspected.
Complications
Severe, intractable neurogenic pain.
Permanent wasting and loss of function of hand muscles.
Vascular: Post-stenotic dilatation of the subclavian artery leading to aneurysm formation, thrombosis, and distal embolization (causing gangrene of the fingertips).
Medical / Conservative Management
First-line treatment for purely neurological, mild symptoms.
Physiotherapy: Specific exercises aimed at strengthening the shoulder girdle muscles (trapezius, levator scapulae) to elevate the shoulder and open up the thoracic outlet space.
Postural correction (avoiding slouching).
NSAIDs for pain relief.
Surgical Management
Indications: Failure of 3-6 months of conservative therapy, progressive muscle wasting, or any signs of vascular compression (ischemia, aneurysm).
Procedure: Surgical excision of the cervical rib (or the fibrous band) along with the division of the scalenus anterior muscle (scaleneotomy). This thoroughly decompresses the brachial plexus and the subclavian artery.

OSTEOMYELITIS — ACUTE AND CHRONIC Definition Osteomyelitis is an inflammatory and infective disease of bone and bone marrow, usually caused by microorganisms, most commonly bacteria, resulting in destruction of bone and surrounding tissues. Acute osteomyelitis: Infection of bone of relatively recent onset, usually associated with acute inflammation and systemic manifestations. Chronic osteomyelitis: Persistent or recurrent bone infection lasting for a prolonged period, usually associated with necrotic bone (sequestrum), new bone formation (involucrum), sinus formation and recurrent discharge. Common Causative Organism Staphylococcus aureus is the most common organism. Other organisms: • • • • • Streptococci Gram-negative bacilli Pseudomonas aeruginosa Salmonella — particularly in sickle-cell disease Mycobacterium tuberculosis — tuberculous osteomyelitis Etiology / Causes 1. 2. 3. Hematogenous spread Microorganisms reach bone through the bloodstream. Common in: ◦ ◦ ◦ ◦ ◦ Children Metaphysis of long bones Femur Tibia Humerus Direct implantation Organisms are introduced directly into bone following: ◦ ◦ ◦ ◦ ◦ Open fractures Penetrating injuries Orthopaedic surgery Internal fixation Joint replacement Contiguous spread Infection spreads from adjacent soft tissue. Examples: ◦ ◦ Diabetic foot ulcer Pressure sore Diseases of Bones and Joints — 20-Mark University Exam Answers Page 1 of 23◦ ◦ Cellulitis Chronic wound Predisposing factors • • • •

--- **1 OSTEOMYELITIS ACUTE AND CHRONIC** **Definition** Osteomyelitis is an inflammatory and infective disease of bone and bone marrow usually caused by microorganisms most commonly bacteria resulting in destruction of bone and surrounding tissues Acute osteomyelitis Infection of bone of relatively recent onset usually associated with acute inflammation and systemic manifestations Chronic osteomyelitis Persistent or recurrent bone infection lasting for a prolonged period usually associated with necrotic bone sequestrum new bone formation involucrum sinus formation and recurrent discharge **Common Causative Organism** Staphylococcus aureus is the most common organism Other organisms • Streptococci • Gram negative bacilli • Pseudomonas aeruginosa • Salmonella particularly in sickle cell disease • Mycobacterium tuberculosis tuberculous osteomyelitis **Etiology / Causes** 1 Hematogenous spread Microorganisms reach bone through the bloodstream Common in ◦ Children ◦ Metaphysis of long bones ◦ Femur ◦ Tibia ◦ Humerus 2 Direct implantation Organisms are introduced directly into bone following ◦ Open fractures ◦ Penetrating injuries ◦ Orthopaedic surgery ◦ Internal fixation ◦ Joint replacement 3 Contiguous spread Infection spreads from adjacent soft tissue Examples ◦ Diabetic foot ulcer ◦ Pressure sore ◦ Cellulitis ◦ Chronic wound **Predisposing factors** • Diabetes mellitus • Immunosuppression • Malnutrition • Peripheral vascular disease • Trauma • Open fracture • Previous surgery • Prosthetic implants • Intravenous drug use • Chronic skin infection **Pathophysiology** Infection → bacterial multiplication → inflammatory response → vascular congestion → edema → increased intraosseous pressure → vascular compromise → bone necrosis → abscess formation → periosteal elevation → sequestrum formation → involucrum formation → sinus formation Important terms Sequestrum Dead separated piece of infected bone Involucrum New bone formed around a seque
<truncated 20191 bytes>
ions** • Joint aspiration when indicated • Synovial fluid analysis • Culture to exclude pyogenic infection **Imaging** • X ray • MRI when required **Neurological evaluation** If Charcot arthropathy is suspected • Sensory examination • Proprioception • Reflexes • Neurological assessment **Differential Diagnosis** • Rheumatoid arthritis • Osteoarthritis • Septic arthritis • Tuberculous arthritis • Gout • Neuropathic arthropathy due to other causes **Complications** • Joint destruction • Deformity • Subluxation • Dislocation • Pathological fracture • Instability • Loss of function • Secondary osteoarthritis • Neurological disability in associated neurosyphilis **Medical Management** **Specific treatment** Penicillin based therapy is the standard treatment for syphilis with the exact preparation dose and duration determined by the stage and clinical form of disease Management may include • Appropriate penicillin therapy • Treatment of sexual partners according to public health guidance • Screening for other sexually transmitted infections • Neurological evaluation when indicated • Analgesics • Management of secondary complications **For Charcot arthropathy** • Protection from excessive loading • Off loading • Appropriate orthosis • Footwear modification where applicable • Management of associated neurological disease **Surgical Management** Surgery is generally directed toward the resulting structural damage rather than eradication of the infection itself May include • Synovectomy in selected cases • Debridement when indicated • Arthrodesis for severe instability • Joint reconstruction • Joint replacement in selected patients after appropriate infection treatment • Correction of severe deformity **Rehabilitation** • Protection of affected joint • Pain management • Maintaining safe ROM • Muscle strengthening • Orthotic support • Gait training • Assistive devices when required • Patient education regarding joint protection

OSTEOMA DEFINITION Osteoma is a benign, slow-growing tumour composed of mature compact or cancellous bone. It commonly occurs in the skull and facial bones and rarely causes malignant transformation. CLASSIFICATION According to site Central osteoma: Develops within bone. Peripheral osteoma: Develops from the surface of bone. According to structure Compact osteoma Cancellous osteoma ETIOLOGY The exact cause is uncertain. Possible factors: Developmental abnormality Genetic factors Previous trauma Chronic irritation Associated syndromes, particularly Gardner syndrome CLINICAL FEATURES Small osteomas may be asymptomatic. When symptomatic: Slow-growing hard swelling Painless mass Facial asymmetry Headache in some cranial lesions Sinus obstruction Visual symptoms if orbital structures are involved Cosmetic deformity • • • • • • • • • • • • • • • • Orthopaedics & Metabolic Bone Diseases — University Exam Notes Page 1 of 17INVESTIGATIONS • • • • X-ray: Dense, well-defined bony lesion CT scan: Best for defining the size, location and relationship to surrounding structures. MRI: Useful when soft-tissue or intracranial involvement is suspected. Biopsy: Usually not required for a typical small osteoma but may be performed when diagnosis is uncertain. COMPLICATIONS • • • • • • Cosmetic deformity Pressure on adjacent structures Sinus obstruction Visual impairment Rare neurological complications Recurrence after incomplete excision MEDICAL MANAGEMENT • • Asymptomatic: Obs
<truncated 29496 bytes>
ain, Vertebral compression fracture, Loss of height, Kyphosis, Fragility fracture (Hip, Wrist, Vertebrae), Difficulty walking, Reduced independence. INVESTIGATIONS • • • DXA scan: Standard test for bone mineral density. T-score thresholds apply (≥ -1.0 Normal, -1.0 to -2.5 Osteopenia, ≤-2.5 Osteoporosis). Blood investigations: Calcium, Phosphate, ALP, Vitamin D, Renal function, Thyroid studies (to identify secondary causes). X-ray: Fractures, Vertebral compression, Kyphosis. COMPLICATIONS Vertebral compression fracture, Hip fracture, Wrist fracture, Loss of height, Kyphosis, Chronic pain, Reduced mobility, Falls, Loss of independence, Increased mortality following major hip fracture. MEDICAL MANAGEMENT 1. 2. Lifestyle management: Adequate calcium & vitamin D, Weight-bearing & resistance exercise, Balance training, Smoking cessation, Limit excessive alcohol, Fall prevention. Pharmacological treatment: Bisphosphonates, Denosumab, Selective estrogen receptor modulators, Anabolic agents (teriparatide/abaloparatide), Romosozumab. Choice depends on fracture risk, age, renal function, previous fractures, contraindications. SURGICAL MANAGEMENT Osteoporosis itself is usually managed medically. Surgery is required for complications (e.g., fracture management via internal fixation, arthroplasty, vertebral augmentation for vertebral compression, hemiarthroplasty/THA for hip fractures). PHYSIOTHERAPY MANAGEMENT Goals: Improve muscle strength, Improve balance, Prevent falls, Maintain posture, Improve functional independence, Reduce fracture risk. Exercises: Weight-bearing exercise, Resistance training, Back extensor strengthening, Balance training, Postural exercises, Gait training. Orthopaedics & Metabolic Bone Diseases — University Exam Notes Page 16 of 17Precautions: Avoid high-impact activity in patients at high fracture risk, Forceful spinal flexion, Excessive twisting, Unsupervised heavy lifting.

--- **1 OSTEOARTHRITIS** **DEFINITION** Osteoarthritis OA is a chronic progressive degenerative disorder of synovial joints characterized by loss of articular cartilage subchondral bone changes osteophyte formation and varying degrees of synovial inflammation resulting in pain stiffness and functional limitation **CAUSES / RISK FACTORS** **Primary OA** Occurs without a single identifiable cause Risk factors • Increasing age • Female sex • Obesity • Genetic predisposition • Previous joint injury • Repetitive joint loading **Secondary OA** Occurs due to an identifiable condition • Previous fracture • Joint infection • Rheumatoid arthritis • Congenital joint abnormality • Ligament injury • Meniscal injury • Avascular necrosis • Metabolic disorders **COMMON SITES** • Knee • Hip • Spine • Hands • First MTP joint • Shoulder and other joints less commonly **PATHOPHYSIOLOGY** Mechanical/biological stress ↓ Cartilage matrix degeneration ↓ Loss of proteoglycans and collagen integrity ↓ Cartilage thinning and fissuring ↓ Subchondral sclerosis ↓ Osteophyte formation ↓ Joint space narrowing ↓ Pain + stiffness + deformity **CLINICAL FEATURES** **Symptoms** • Joint pain usually worse with activity • Pain relieved by rest initially • Morning stiffness usually less than 30 minutes • Stiffness after prolonged inactivity • Difficulty walking • Difficulty climbing stairs • Reduced functional activity **Signs** • Joint tenderness • Crepitus • Reduced ROM • Bony enlargement • Joint line tenderness • Muscle weakness • Deformity in advanced disease **Hand OA** • Heberdens nodes DIP joints • Bouchards nodes PIP joints **DEFORMITIES** **Knee** • Varus deformity • Valgus deformity **Hip** • Flexion deformity • External rotation • Adduction deformity **Hands** • Heberdens and Bouchards nodes **RADIOLOGICAL FEATURES** X ray shows • Non uniform joint space narrowing • Osteophytes • Subchondral sclerosis • Subchondral cysts • Bony remode
<truncated 25516 bytes>
 • Genetic predisposition • Autoimmune dysregulation • Environmental triggers **CLINICAL FEATURES** **Musculoskeletal** • Polyarthritis • Arthralgia • Myalgia • Proximal muscle weakness **Vascular** • Raynaud phenomenon **Skin** • Puffy fingers • Sclerodactyly like changes • Rash **Other** • Dysphagia • Esophageal dysfunction • Interstitial lung disease • Pulmonary hypertension • Fatigue **DEFORMITIES** May develop • Joint deformity • Contractures • Sclerodactyly • Muscle wasting • Reduced hand function **COMPLICATIONS** • Pulmonary hypertension • Interstitial lung disease • Esophageal dysfunction • Cardiac involvement • Renal involvement usually less prominent than in classic SLE • Severe muscle weakness • Functional disability **RADIOLOGICAL FEATURES** No single pathognomonic radiographic appearance May demonstrate • Joint space narrowing • Soft tissue swelling • Erosive arthritis in some patients • Interstitial lung disease on chest X ray/HRCT • Muscle inflammation/edema on MRI **INVESTIGATIONS** **Blood** • ANA • Anti U1 RNP • CBC • ESR/CRP • CK • Renal and liver function **Other** • Pulmonary function tests • Echocardiography • HRCT chest where indicated • MRI muscle if myositis is suspected **CONSERVATIVE / MEDICAL MANAGEMENT** Treatment depends on the dominant manifestations May include • NSAIDs • Corticosteroids • Hydroxychloroquine • Methotrexate • Other immunosuppressive drugs • Vasodilators for Raynaud phenomenon • Treatment for pulmonary hypertension • Treatment of interstitial lung disease **Physiotherapy** • ROM exercises • Muscle strengthening • Aerobic conditioning • Breathing exercises • Hand therapy • Joint protection • Energy conservation • Functional training **SURGICAL MANAGEMENT** Usually not required for the primary disease May be considered for • Severe joint destruction • Fixed deformity • Contractures • Digital complications • Joint replacement in selected cases

CERVICOBRACHIAL SYNDROME Definition Cervicobrachial syndrome is a clinical condition characterized by pain, paraesthesia, numbness, weakness or other neurological symptoms originating from the cervical spine or surrounding structures and radiating into the shoulder, arm, forearm or hand. Causes / Etiology • Cervical intervertebral disc prolapse • Cervical spondylosis and osteophytes • Foraminal or spinal canal stenosis • Whiplash and other cervical trauma • Muscle spasm and postural dysfunction • Brachial plexus lesions • Tumour, infection or inflammatory disorders • Thoracic outlet disorders Pathophysiology Cervical pathology → nerve-root irritation/compression → inflammation and impaired nerve conduction → pain and paraesthesia → weakness and functional limitation. Clinical Features • Neck pain with radiation to shoulder/upper limb • Burning, shooting or electric-shock-like pain • Paraesthesia and numbness in a dermatomal pattern • Muscle weakness and reduced grip strength • Reduced deep tendon reflexes corresponding to the affected root • Restricted cervical ROM and muscle spasm • Symptoms may worsen with cervical movement or compression Investigations • Cervical X-ray for degenerative or bony changes • MRI for disc pathology, nerve-root or cord compression • CT for bony/foraminal abnormalities • EMG/NCV when nerve involvement needs clarification • Neurological examination of dermatomes, myotomes and reflexes Complications • Chronic pain Page 1• Persistent sensory deficit • Motor weakness and muscle wasting • Loss of hand function • Reduced work capacity • Cervical myelopathy in severe cord compression Conservative / Medical Management • Relative rest and activity modification • Analgesics/NSAIDs as indicated • Muscle relaxants in selected cases • Neuropathic pain 
<truncated 13310 bytes>
th or without radiating symptoms. Clinical Features • Deep buttock pain • Pain radiating into posterior thigh • Sciatic-type symptoms • Paraesthesia or numbness • Pain aggravated by prolonged sitting • Tenderness over piriformis • Pain with resisted hip external rotation • Pain with piriformis stretching • Possible reduced hip ROM Special Tests • FAIR test • Freiberg sign • Pace sign • Beatty manoeuvre Differential Diagnosis • Lumbar radiculopathy • Lumbar disc prolapse • Sacroiliac joint dysfunction • Hip pathology • Hamstring pathology • Spinal stenosis Investigations Page 13• Primarily a clinical diagnosis • MRI lumbar spine or pelvis to exclude other pathology • EMG/NCV in selected cases • Ultrasound in selected cases • Diagnostic local injection in selected persistent cases Complications • Chronic buttock pain • Persistent sciatic symptoms • Reduced walking and sitting tolerance • Sleep disturbance • Reduced athletic performance • Functional disability Conservative / Physiotherapy Management • Activity modification and avoidance of prolonged sitting • Heat/cold or TENS when appropriate • Piriformis, hip-rotator and gluteal stretching • Gluteus maximus and medius strengthening • Core strengthening • Neural mobilization when appropriate • Gait/running biomechanics correction • Gradual return to sport • Analgesics/NSAIDs when appropriate • Selected image-guided injection for persistent symptoms Surgical Management • Rarely required • Considered after appropriate conservative treatment in severe persistent cases with convincing evidence of sciatic nerve compression • Piriformis release • Sciatic nerve decompression • Correction of associated structural abnormalities Conclusion A careful differential diagnosis is essential because lumbar radiculopathy and other hip/spinal disorders can mimic piriformis syndrome.
</USER_REQUEST>

--- **1 CERVICOBRACHIAL SYNDROME** **Definition** Cervicobrachial syndrome is a clinical condition characterized by pain paraesthesia numbness weakness or other neurological symptoms originating from the cervical spine or surrounding structures and radiating into the shoulder arm forearm or hand **Causes / Etiology** • Cervical intervertebral disc prolapse • Cervical spondylosis and osteophytes • Foraminal or spinal canal stenosis • Whiplash and other cervical trauma • Muscle spasm and postural dysfunction • Brachial plexus lesions • Tumour infection or inflammatory disorders • Thoracic outlet disorders **Pathophysiology** Cervical pathology → nerve root irritation/compression → inflammation and impaired nerve conduction → pain and paraesthesia → weakness and functional limitation **Clinical Features** • Neck pain with radiation to shoulder/upper limb • Burning shooting or electric shock like pain • Paraesthesia and numbness in a dermatomal pattern • Muscle weakness and reduced grip strength • Reduced deep tendon reflexes corresponding to the affected root • Restricted cervical ROM and muscle spasm • Symptoms may worsen with cervical movement or compression **Investigations** • Cervical X ray for degenerative or bony changes • MRI for disc pathology nerve root or cord compression • CT for bony/foraminal abnormalities • EMG/NCV when nerve involvement needs clarification • Neurological examination of dermatomes myotomes and reflexes **Complications** • Chronic pain • Persistent sensory deficit • Motor weakness and muscle wasting • Loss of hand function • Reduced work capacity • Cervical myelopathy in severe cord compression **Conservative / Medical Management** • Relative rest and activity modification • Analgesics/NSAIDs as indicated • Muscle relaxants in selected cases • Neuropathic pain medication when appropriate • Selected use of cervical collar in acute cases **Physiotherapy Management** • Pain relief with TENS or thermal modalities when 
<truncated 13159 bytes>
variation → sciatic nerve irritation/compression → local inflammation → buttock pain with or without radiating symptoms **Clinical Features** • Deep buttock pain • Pain radiating into posterior thigh • Sciatic type symptoms • Paraesthesia or numbness • Pain aggravated by prolonged sitting • Tenderness over piriformis • Pain with resisted hip external rotation • Pain with piriformis stretching • Possible reduced hip ROM **Special Tests** • FAIR test • Freiberg sign • Pace sign • Beatty manoeuvre **Differential Diagnosis** • Lumbar radiculopathy • Lumbar disc prolapse • Sacroiliac joint dysfunction • Hip pathology • Hamstring pathology • Spinal stenosis **Investigations** • Primarily a clinical diagnosis • MRI lumbar spine or pelvis to exclude other pathology • EMG/NCV in selected cases • Ultrasound in selected cases • Diagnostic local injection in selected persistent cases **Complications** • Chronic buttock pain • Persistent sciatic symptoms • Reduced walking and sitting tolerance • Sleep disturbance • Reduced athletic performance • Functional disability **Conservative / Physiotherapy Management** • Activity modification and avoidance of prolonged sitting • Heat/cold or TENS when appropriate • Piriformis hip rotator and gluteal stretching • Gluteus maximus and medius strengthening • Core strengthening • Neural mobilization when appropriate • Gait/running biomechanics correction • Gradual return to sport • Analgesics/NSAIDs when appropriate • Selected image guided injection for persistent symptoms **Surgical Management** • Rarely required • Considered after appropriate conservative treatment in severe persistent cases with convincing evidence of sciatic nerve compression • Piriformis release • Sciatic nerve decompression • Correction of associated structural abnormalities **Conclusion** A careful differential diagnosis is essential because lumbar radiculopathy and other hip/spinal disorders can mimic piriformis syndrome

SPECIAL TESTS — LUMBO-SACRAL REGION I. TEST FOR LUMBAR INSTABILITY 1. PASSIVE LUMBAR EXTENSION TEST PURPOSE To assess lumbar spine instability. PROCEDURE • • • • Patient lies prone. Examiner lifts both the patient’s legs together by holding around the ankles. The legs are elevated while the knees remain extended. Observe the patient’s symptoms and lumbar response. POSITIVE SIGN • • Development/reproduction of low-back pain Feeling of heaviness or instability in the lumbar region. INDICATES Lumbar segmental instability. II. TESTS FOR MUSCLE DYSFUNCTION 2. 90–90 STRAIGHT LEG RAISING TEST PURPOSE To assess hamstring muscle tightness/flexibility. PROCEDURE • • • • Patient lies supine. Flex the hip and knee to approximately 90°. Stabilize the thigh. Slowly extend the knee while maintaining hip flexion. POSITIVE SIGN • • Inability to achieve full knee extension. Increased resistance/tightness in the posterior thigh. INDICATES Hamstring tightness/shortness. 3. TRENDELENBURG TEST PURPOSE To assess hip abductor muscle function, especially gluteus medius. PROCEDURE • • • Patient stands on one leg. Ask the patient to lift the opposite foot from the floor. Observe the position of the pelvis. POSITIVE SIGN • Pelvis drops on the side of the lifted/non-weight-bearing leg. INDICATES Weakness of the hip abductors of the weight-bearing leg, particularly gluteus medius.III. TESTS FOR NEUROLOGICAL DYSFUNCTION 4. STRAIGHT LEG RAISING TEST (SLR/LASÈGUE’S TEST) PURPOSE To assess lumbosacral nerve-root irritation, particularly due to PIVD. PROCEDURE • • • • Patient lies supine. Keep the knee fully extended. Slowly raise the patient’s straight leg by flexing the hip. Note the angle and symptoms produced. POSITIVE SIGN • Reproduction of radicular/sciatic pain, typically between about 30–70° of hip flexion. INDICATES Lumbosacral nerve-root irritation/compression, commonly associated with PIVD. 5. SLUMP TEST PURPOSE To assess neural tissue mechanosensitivity, particul
<truncated 1200 bytes>
e. Place the tested leg in: Hip flexion Abduction External rotation ◦ Place the lateral ankle over the opposite thigh. Stabilize the opposite pelvis. Gently press the flexed knee downward. One hip and knee are maximally flexed toward the chest. The opposite leg is allowed to hang off the edge of the table. Apply gentle pressure to produce opposite movements at the pelvis. POSITIVE SIGN • Reproduction of pain in the sacroiliac region. INDICATES Sacroiliac joint dysfunction. 9. GILLET’S TEST PURPOSE To assess sacroiliac joint mobility. PROCEDURE • • • • Patient stands. POSITIVE SIGN • Abnormal or restricted movement of the PSIS compared with the opposite side. INDICATES Sacroiliac joint hypomobility/dysfunction. 10. MARCH’S TEST PURPOSE To assess sacroiliac joint movement/dysfunction. PROCEDURE • • • • Patient stands. Examiner palpates the PSIS. Patient marches by lifting one knee at a time. ---**I TESTS FOR LIGAMENT STABILITY** ---**1 ANTERIOR DRAWER TEST ANKLE** PURPOSE To assess the integrity of the anterior talofibular ligament ATFL and lateral ankle stability PROCEDURE • Patient sits or lies supine • Keep the ankle in a neutral or slight plantar flexed position • Stabilize the distal tibia with one hand • Grasp the heel/talus with the other hand • Draw the foot/talus anteriorly POSITIVE SIGN • Excessive anterior translation compared with the opposite ankle • Soft/absent end feel • Pain may be reproduced INDICATES ATFL injury/lateral ankle instability ---**2 TALAR TILT TEST** PURPOSE To assess the integrity of the lateral ankle ligaments particularly the calcaneofibular ligament CFL PROCEDURE • Patient sits or lies supine • Stabilize the distal leg • Grasp the calcaneus/heel • Tilt the calcaneus into inversion POSITIVE SIGN • Excessive inversion compared with the opposite side • Pain or a soft end feel INDICATES CFL injury/lateral ankle instability ---**II TESTS FOR VASCULAR CONDITIONS DVT** ---**3 HOMANS TEST** PURPOSE Traditionally used to assess for deep vein thrombosis DVT of the lower limb PROCEDURE • Patient lies supine • Knee is extended • Examiner supports the leg • Passively dorsiflex the ankle POSITIVE SIGN • Pain in the calf during passive ankle dorsiflexion INDICATES Traditionally considered suggestive of DVT ---**4 MOSES TEST** PURPOSE Traditionally used to assess for DVT PROCEDURE • Patient lies supine with the leg relaxed • Compress the calf anteroposteriorly • Then compress the calf laterally POSITIVE SIGN • Pain produced by anteroposterior compression but not by lateral compression INDICATES Traditionally considered suggestive of DVT

### 🤖 Antigravity
Here are the comprehensive, 20-mark university exam answers for both topics, formulated from the perspective of an expert physiotherapist. 

While David J. Magee’s renowned textbook, *"Orthopedic Physical Assessment,"* is primarily utilized for musculoskeletal conditions, its foundational framework for sequential assessment (Subjective History → Observation → Palpation → Measurements/Special Tests) is the gold standard across all physiotherapy disciplines. The following answers apply Magee's systematic, evidence-based assessment principles to the evaluation of cardiovascular and pulmonary dysfunction.

---

# **TOPIC A: Subjective Examination (20 Marks)**

**Definition:** 
Subjective Examination is the systematic process of gathering clinical data directly from the patient or their caregivers. As emphasized in David J. Magee's assessment framework, the subjective history is the most critical component of the evaluation, often providing up to 80% of the information necessary to formulate a clinical diagnosis. It guides the subsequent objective examination and helps the physiotherapist understand the patient's functional limitations.

### **1. Demographic Data (2 Marks)**
*   **Definition:** The statistical characteristics of a patient.
*   **Clinical Significance:**
    *   **Age:** Certain conditions are age-specific (e.g., COPD and coronary artery disease are more common in older adults, whereas asthma or cystic fibrosis may present in childhood).
    *   **Gender:** Males have a statistically higher risk for early-onset ischemic heart disease. 
    *   **Occupation:** Helps identify exposure to occupational hazards.

### **2. Chief Complaint (2 Marks)**
*   **Definition:** The primary symptom or problem that prompted the patient to seek physiotherapy, recorded in the patient’s own words.
*   **Clinical Significance:** Helps prioritize the treatment plan. Common cardiopulmonary chief complaints include dyspnea (shortness of breath), angina (chest pain), fatigue, claudication (leg pain on walking), and 
<truncated 6332 bytes>
) indicates infection; rust-colored indicates lobar pneumonia; frothy-pink indicates pulmonary edema.

### **4. Palpation and Percussion (3 Marks)**
*   **Tactile and Vocal Fremitus:** The vibration felt on the chest wall when the patient speaks (e.g., saying "99"). 
    *   *Increased fremitus* indicates consolidation (e.g., pneumonia). 
    *   *Decreased fremitus* indicates increased air or fluid in the pleural space (e.g., pneumothorax, pleural effusion).
*   **Thoracic Mobility:** Palpating the ribs and thoracic spine for segmental mobility, stiffness, and pain.
*   **Percussion:** Tapping the chest wall to determine underlying tissue density.
    *   *Resonant:* Normal air-filled lungs.
    *   *Hyper-resonant:* Hyperinflation (COPD) or pneumothorax.
    *   *Dull/Stony Dull:* Solid organ, consolidation, or pleural effusion.

### **5. Auscultation (3 Marks)**
Listening to the internal sounds of the body using a stethoscope.
*   **Breath Sounds:** 
    *   *Normal:* Vesicular (soft, low-pitched over peripheral lung fields), Bronchial (harsh, over trachea).
    *   *Adventitious (Abnormal):* Crackles/Crepitations (fluid in alveoli/airways), Wheezes (bronchospasm/narrowing), Pleural friction rub (pleurisy).
*   **Heart Sounds:** 
    *   *Normal:* S1 (AV valve closure) and S2 (Semilunar valve closure).
    *   *Abnormal:* S3 gallop (indicates heart failure/volume overload), murmurs (valvular stenosis or regurgitation).

### **6. Measurements (3 Marks)**
*   **Chest Expansion:** Measured using a standard inch-tape at three levels: Axillary, Nipple, and Xiphisternum. Normal excursion is 3-5 cm. Decreased or asymmetrical expansion indicates restrictive lung diseases, lobar collapse, or unilateral paralysis.
*   **BMI and Waist-Hip Ratio:** 
    *   *BMI:* Weight (kg) / Height (m²). 
    *   *Waist-Hip Ratio:* Evaluates central obesity. Both are critical anthropometric measurements used to assess the risk of metabolic syndrome and cardiovascular disease. High ratios correlate directly with ischemic heart disease.

**Assessment of Cardiovascular and Pulmonary Dysfunction** --- **TOPIC A Subjective Examination** **Definition** Subjective Examination is the systematic process of gathering clinical data directly from the patient or their caregivers As emphasized in David J Magees assessment framework the subjective history is the most critical component of the evaluation often providing up to 80 percent of the information necessary to formulate a clinical diagnosis It guides the subsequent objective examination and helps the physiotherapist understand the patients functional limitations **1 Demographic Data** • Definition The statistical characteristics of a patient • Clinical Significance • Age Certain conditions are age specific eg COPD and coronary artery disease are more common in older adults whereas asthma or cystic fibrosis may present in childhood • Gender Males have a statistically higher risk for early onset ischemic heart disease • Occupation Helps identify exposure to occupational hazards **2 Chief Complaint** • Definition The primary symptom or problem that prompted the patient to seek physiotherapy recorded in the patients own words • Clinical Significance Helps prioritize the treatment plan Common cardiopulmonary chief complaints include dyspnea shortness of breath angina chest pain fatigue claudication leg pain on walking and chronic cough **3 History of Present Illness HPI and Functional Limitation** • Definition A detailed chronological account of the chief complaint from its onset to the present • Clinical Significance • Onset and Duration Is the dyspnea ASS OF CARDIO ---1. Demographic Data Definition: The statistical characteristics of a patient.
•	Clinical Significance:
•	Age: Certain conditions are age-specific (e.g., COPD and coronary artery disease are more common in older adults, whereas asthma or cystic fibrosis may present in childhood).
•	Gender: Males have a statistically higher risk for early-onset ischemic heart disease.
•	Occupation: Helps identify exposure to occupational hazards.
2. Chief Complaint 
•	Definition: The primary symptom or problem that prompted the patient to seek physiotherapy, recorded in the patient’s own words.
•	Clinical Significance: Helps prioritize the treatment plan. Common cardiopulmonary chief complaints include dyspnea (shortness of breath), angina (chest pain), fatigue, claudication (leg pain on walking), and chronic cough.
3. History of Present Illness (HPI) & Functional Limitation Definition: A detailed chronological account of the chief complaint from its onset to the present.
•	Clinical Significance:
•	Onset and Duration: Is the dyspnea sudden (e.g., pulmonary embolism, pneumothorax) or gradual (e.g., COPD, congestive heart failure)?
•	Aggravating and Relieving Factors: Orthopnea (breathlessness on lying flat) relieved by sitting up suggests congestive heart failure. Angina relieved by rest suggests stable coronary disease.
•	Functional Limitation: Evaluating how the illness impacts Activities of Daily Living (ADLs). The physiotherapist must quantify this using scales (e.g., NYHA Functional Classification for heart failure or the MRC Dyspnea Scale). Example: "Patient is unable to climb one flight of stairs without severe breathlessness."
4. Past Medical and Surgical History 
•	Definition: A record of all previous illnesses, hospitalizations, and surgeries.
•	Clinical Significance:
•	Medical: History of hypertension, diabetes mellitus, asthma, or previous myocardial infarction (MI) significantly alters the risk profile and exercise prescription.
•	Surgical: P
<truncated 4490 bytes>
e. Purulent (yellow/green) indicates infection; rust-colored indicates lobar pneumonia; frothy-pink indicates pulmonary edema.
4. Palpation and Percussion Tactile and Vocal Fremitus: The vibration felt on the chest wall when the patient speaks (e.g., saying "99").
•	Increased fremitus indicates consolidation (e.g., pneumonia).
•	Decreased fremitus indicates increased air or fluid in the pleural space (e.g., pneumothorax, pleural effusion).
•	Thoracic Mobility: Palpating the ribs and thoracic spine for segmental mobility, stiffness, and pain.
•	Percussion: Tapping the chest wall to determine underlying tissue density.
•	Resonant: Normal air-filled lungs.
•	Hyper-resonant: Hyperinflation (COPD) or pneumothorax.
•	Dull/Stony Dull: Solid organ, consolidation, or pleural effusion.
5. Auscultation 
Listening to the internal sounds of the body using a stethoscope.
•	Breath Sounds:
•	Normal: Vesicular (soft, low-pitched over peripheral lung fields), Bronchial (harsh, over trachea).
•	Adventitious (Abnormal): Crackles/Crepitations (fluid in alveoli/airways), Wheezes (bronchospasm/narrowing), Pleural friction rub (pleurisy).
•	Heart Sounds:
•	Normal: S1 (AV valve closure) and S2 (Semilunar valve closure).
•	Abnormal: S3 gallop (indicates heart failure/volume overload), murmurs (valvular stenosis or regurgitation).
6. Measurements Chest Expansion: Measured using a standard inch-tape at three levels: Axillary, Nipple, and Xiphisternum. Normal excursion is 3-5 cm. Decreased or asymmetrical expansion indicates restrictive lung diseases, lobar collapse, or unilateral paralysis.
•	BMI and Waist-Hip Ratio:
•	BMI: Weight (kg) / Height (m²).
•	Waist-Hip Ratio: Evaluates central obesity. Both are critical anthropometric measurements used to assess the risk of metabolic syndrome and cardiovascular disease. High ratios correlate directly with ischemic heart disease.

### 🤖 Antigravity
**Functional Assessment for Cardiovascular and Pulmonary Dysfunction** **Definition of Functional Assessment** Functional assessment evaluates an individuals capacity to perform activities of daily living and exercise determining the impact of cardiovascular and pulmonary dysfunction on overall quality of life and physical capability It provides objective data for exercise prescription and monitors disease progression or recovery **Borg Scale for Rate of Perceived Exertion RPE** • **Definition** The Borg RPE scale is a subjective tool used to measure an individuals perceived effort and fatigue during physical activity • **Scale Range** It ranges from 6 to 20 where 6 represents no exertion at all and 20 represents maximal exertion • **Theoretical Basis** The scale is designed to correlate with the individuals heart rate multiplied by 10 eg a rating of 12 corresponds roughly to a heart rate of 120 beats per minute • **Clinical Significance** It is crucial for patients taking medications like beta blockers which alter heart rate response allowing them to self monitor exercise intensity safely **Modified Borg Scale** • **Definition** A revised version of the original Borg scale that uses a non linear scale from 0 to 10 • **Scale Ratings** 0 is rest or no breathlessness 3 is moderate breathlessness 5 is severe and 10 is maximal breathlessness • **Clinical Application** It is highly sensitive to changes in dyspnea and leg fatigue making it the preferred scale for patients with chronic obstructive pulmonary disease COPD and heart failure during rehabilitation **Grades of Dyspnea** Dyspnea or shortness of breath is a primary limiting factor in cardiopulmonary diseases **Modified Medical Research Council mMRC Dyspnea Scale** • **Definition** A simple grading system that assesses the degree of baseline breathlessness related to physical activity • **Grade 0** I only get breathless with strenuous exercise • **Grade 1** I get short of breath when hurrying on the level or walking up a slight hill • **Gra
<truncated 3409 bytes>
* A laboratory based test using a treadmill with continuous ECG blood pressure and symptom monitoring to evaluate cardiac ischemia and maximal functional capacity **Theoretical Bases of Different Protocols for Maximal Exercise Testing** Maximal exercise protocols vary in their starting intensity and rate of progression to accommodate different fitness levels and clinical conditions **Bruce Protocol** • **Theoretical Basis** A maximal multi stage treadmill protocol characterized by large abrupt increases in workload It is designed to quickly bring healthy subjects or athletes to their maximal effort • **Procedure** Speed and incline increase every 3 minutes starting at 1 7 miles per hour mph and a 10 percent grade • **Clinical Application** Best suited for younger or physically active individuals being evaluated for coronary artery disease **Modified Bruce Protocol** • **Theoretical Basis** Designed for older adults or patients with known cardiac disease who cannot tolerate the initial high workload of the standard Bruce protocol • **Procedure** It adds two preliminary stages stage 1 starts at 1 7 mph with 0 percent grade and stage 2 is 1 7 mph with 5 percent grade • **Clinical Application** Prevents premature test termination due to musculoskeletal limitations or sudden cardiac stress allowing a safer and more gradual cardiovascular adaptation **Balke Protocol** • **Theoretical Basis** A constant speed protocol with gradual incremental increases in workload primarily driven by elevation changes rather than speed • **Procedure** The treadmill speed is kept constant usually 3 3 mph while the grade increases by 1 percent every minute • **Clinical Application** Highly effective for elderly patients deconditioned individuals and those with peripheral arterial disease PAD because the constant walking speed reduces the risk of tripping and provides a steady linear increase in myocardial oxygen demand --- **Book Reference** David J Maggi Functional Assessment for Cardiovascular and Pulmonary dysfunction

Chief complaint, History of Present illness including functional limitation, Past Medical Surgical history, Family history, Occupational & Personal history, Socio-Economic, Environmental and Psychological History, Drug and Treatment History///// musculoskeletal —-Vital Parameters
 Temperature: State route used (axillary/oral) and presence of fever.
 Normal Value: 98.6°F (37°C) (Range: 97.8°F – 99.1°F / 36.5°C – 37.3°C)
 Respiratory Rate (RR): Count rate, rhythm, depth, and pattern (thoraco-abdominal in females, abdomino-thoracic in males).
 Normal Value: 12–20 breaths/min, regular, quiet, and unlabored.
 Peripheral Pulses: Palpate radial, dorsalis pedis, or posterior tibial; document rate, rhythm, volume, and bilateral symmetry.
 Normal Value: 60–100 beats/min, regular, Grade 2+ (normal, palpable bilaterally).
 Blood Pressure (BP): Record systolic/diastolic pressure, patient position (sitting/supine), and arm used.
 Normal Value: <120/80 mmHg (Systolic: 100–120 mmHg, Diastolic: 60–80 mmHg).
General Musculoskeletal Observations
 Body Built: Document somatotype (ectomorphic, mesomorphic, endomorphic) and calculated BMI.
 Normal Finding: Mesomorphic build; BMI 18.5–24.9 kg/m².
 Attitude of Limb: Note resting spatial position of the limb relative to anatomical neutral (e.g., antalgic posturing, guarding).
 Normal Finding: Symmetrical, resting comfortably in anatomical neutral without protective spasm.
 Bony Contours: Inspect continuity, prominence, and bilateral symmetry of standard bony landmarks.
 Normal Finding: Intact, smooth, and symmetrical, without step-offs, spurs, or abnormal prominence.
 Deformities: Identify structural (fixed) vs. postural (flexible) deformities, plane of deviation, and joint involved.
 Normal Finding: Absent; normal physiological axial and appendicular alignment.
 Trophic Changes: Assess skin texture, shine, hair loss, nail changes, pigmentation, or signs of autonomic dysfunction.
 Normal Fi
<truncated 18748 bytes>
ing tape used across three levels, measuring the difference from maximal expiration to maximal inspiration:
 Axillary Level: 2nd–3rd intercostal space.
 Mammary / Nipple Level: 4th intercostal space.
 Xiphisternal Level: 8th–10th rib margin / xiphisternal junction.
 Normal Value: 3\text{--}5\text{ cm} (1.5\text{--}2\text{ inches}) difference between full expiration and full inspiration across all levels.
 Symmetry of Chest Movement:
 Method: Examiner places hands firmly on anterior/posterior chest wall with thumbs pinching a midline skin fold at the upper lobes (clavicular region), middle lobes (axillary/nipple), and lower lobes (10th ribs).
 Normal Value: Bilateral, symmetrical, and simultaneous excursion; thumbs diverge equally from the midline by \sim 2\text{ cm} on each side during deep inspiration.
Anthropometric Measurements
 Body Mass Index (BMI):
 Formula: \text{Weight (kg)} / [\text{Height (m)}]^2
 Classifications (WHO):
 Underweight: <18.5\text{ kg/m}^2
 Normal: 18.5\text{--}24.9\text{ kg/m}^2
 Overweight: 25.0\text{--}29.9\text{ kg/m}^2
 Obese: \ge 30.0\text{ kg/m}^2 (Asian cut-off for obesity: \ge 25.0\text{ kg/m}^2)
 Waist-to-Hip Ratio (WHR):
 Method: Waist measured at the narrowest circumference between the lower rib margin and iliac crest (umbilicus level); Hip measured at the widest horizontal circumference over the buttocks/greater trochanters.
 Formula: \text{Waist Circumference} / \text{Hip Circumference}
 Normal Values (Low Cardiovascular / Metabolic Risk):
 Males: <0.90 (Central obesity: \ge 0.90)
 Females: <0.85 (Central obesity: \ge 0.85)

Magee, Orthopedic Physical Assessment (assessment principles). Simplified study notes; not verbatim textbook content. Contents • 1. Blood Investigations and Markers • 2. Ultrasonography (USG) • 3. X-ray (Radiography) • 4. Computed Tomography (CT Scan) • 5. Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI) Page 1 BPT Physiotherapy Study Notes1. Blood Investigations and Markers Definition: Blood investigations are laboratory tests performed on a blood sample to detect inflammation, infection, autoimmune disease, muscle injury, and metabolic problems affecting bones and joints. In simple words: Blood tests help identify whether a musculoskeletal problem may be due to infection, inflammation, an immune disorder, or a metabolic problem. Aims • Detect infection and inflammation. • Identify autoimmune diseases such as rheumatoid arthritis. • Assess bone and mineral metabolism. • Detect muscle damage. • Support diagnosis and differential diagnosis. • Monitor disease activity and response to treatment. Important blood investigations • CBC (Complete Blood Count): Measures red cells, white cells, hemoglobin, and platelets. Increased WBCs may suggest infection; low hemoglobin may indicate anemia; platelets may rise during inflammation. • ESR (Erythrocyte Sedimentation Rate): Measures how far red blood cells settle in one hour. It may rise in inflammatory disorders and infections. It is a nonspecific marker and cannot identify the exact disease. • CRP (C-Reactive Protein): A protein produced mainly by the liver during inflammation. It rises in infection and inflammatory disorders and is useful for monitoring treatment response. It usually changes faster than ESR. • RF (Rheumatoid Factor): An autoantibody that may be present in rheumatoid arthritis. It supports diagnosis but can also be positive in other conditions or some healthy older adults. • Anti-
<truncated 8112 bytes>
the MRI table. • Acquire images in different planes and sequences. • Use contrast material only when indicated. Indications • Ligaments: ACL, PCL, collateral ligament, and selected shoulder injuries. • Tendons and muscles: Rotator cuff or Achilles tears, muscle tears, hematomas, and tendinopathy. • Joints: Meniscal tears, cartilage injury, effusion, synovitis, and osteonecrosis. • Spine: Disc herniation, spinal stenosis, nerve-root compression, spinal cord abnormalities, and infection. • Tumors: Extent of bone marrow and surrounding soft-tissue involvement. Possible findings • Ligament or tendon tears. • Muscle injury and cartilage damage. • Bone marrow edema. • Disc herniation or nerve compression. • Soft-tissue masses. Advantages • No ionizing radiation. • Excellent soft-tissue contrast. • Shows bone marrow abnormalities. • Useful for ligaments, tendons, cartilage, and spinal structures. • Images can be obtained in multiple planes. Limitations • Expensive and often takes longer than X-ray or CT. • Claustrophobia may be a problem. • Some implants or metallic foreign bodies need special safety assessment. Page 8 BPT Physiotherapy Study Notes• Movement can reduce image quality. • Some abnormalities also occur in people without symptoms. Physiotherapy importance: Supports assessment of soft-tissue and spinal conditions, helps plan rehabilitation precautions, and may guide referral. MRI findings must be matched with symptoms and examination. Conclusion: MRI is valuable for soft-tissue, bone marrow, and spinal disorders and does not use ionizing radiation.

iled images of body tissues In simple words MRI is especially useful for muscles ligaments tendons cartilage bone marrow and spinal structures **Principle** • Hydrogen nuclei respond to a strong magnetic field • Radiofrequency pulses change their alignment temporarily • As they return they produce signals • A computer converts these signals into detailed images **Procedure** • Screen the patient for MRI safety including implants and metallic objects • Position the patient on the MRI table • Acquire images in different planes and sequences • Use contrast material only when indicated **Indications** • Ligaments ACL PCL collateral ligament and selected shoulder injuries • Tendons and muscles Rotator cuff or Achilles tears muscle tears hematomas and tendinopathy • Joints Meniscal tears cartilage injury effusion synovitis and osteonecrosis • Spine Disc herniation spinal stenosis nerve root compression spinal cord abnormalities and infection • Tumors Extent of bone marrow and surrounding soft tissue involvement **Possible findings** • Ligament or tendon tears • Muscle injury and cartilage damage • Bone marrow edema • Disc herniation or nerve compression • Soft tissue masses **Advantages** • No ionizing radiation • Excellent soft tissue contrast • Shows bone marrow abnormalities • Useful for ligaments tendons cartilage and spinal structures • Images can be obtained in multiple planes **Limitations** • Expensive and often takes longer than X ray or CT • Claustrophobia may be a problem • Some implants or metallic foreign bodies need special safety assessment • Movement can reduce image quality • Some abnormalities also occur in people without symptoms **Physiotherapy importance** Supports assessment of soft tissue and spinal conditions helps plan rehabilitation precautions and may guide referral MRI findings must be matched with symptoms and examination **Conclusion** MRI is valuable for soft tissue bone marrow and spinal disorders and does not use ionizing radiation

iled images of body tissues In simple words MRI is especially useful for muscles ligaments tendons cartilage bone marrow and spinal structures **Principle** • Hydrogen nuclei respond to a strong magnetic field • Radiofrequency pulses change their alignment temporarily • As they return they produce signals • A computer converts these signals into detailed images **Procedure** • Screen the patient for MRI safety including implants and metallic objects • Position the patient on the MRI table • Acquire images in different planes and sequences • Use contrast material only when indicated **Indications** • Ligaments ACL PCL collateral ligament and selected shoulder injuries • Tendons and muscles Rotator cuff or Achilles tears muscle tears hematomas and tendinopathy • Joints Meniscal tears cartilage injury effusion synovitis and osteonecrosis • Spine Disc herniation spinal stenosis nerve root compression spinal cord abnormalities and infection • Tumors Extent of bone marrow and surrounding soft tissue involvement **Possible findings** • Ligament or tendon tears • Muscle injury and cartilage damage • Bone marrow edema • Disc herniation or nerve compression • Soft tissue masses **Advantages** • No ionizing radiation • Excellent soft tissue contrast • Shows bone marrow abnormalities • Useful for ligaments tendons cartilage and spinal structures • Images can be obtained in multiple planes **Limitations** • Expensive and often takes longer than X ray or CT • Claustrophobia may be a problem • Some implants or metallic foreign bodies need special safety assessment • Movement can reduce image quality • Some abnormalities also occur in people without symptoms **Physiotherapy importance** Supports assessment of soft tissue and spinal conditions helps plan rehabilitation precautions and may guide referral MRI findings must be matched with symptoms and examination **Conclusion** MRI is valuable for soft tissue bone marrow and spinal disorders and does not use ionizing radiation
