### 🧑 You
<USER_REQUEST>
SPECIAL TESTS I. TESTS FOR CIRCULATION & SWELLING 1. ALLEN TEST Purpose To assess the patency of the radial and ulnar arteries. Procedure • • • • Patient clenches the fist. Compress both radial and ulnar arteries. Patient opens the hand. Release one artery at a time and observe colour return. Positive Sign Delayed or absent return of normal hand colour. Indicates Arterial insufficiency or vascular occlusion. 2. DIGITAL BLOOD FLOW TEST Purpose To assess circulation to the fingers. Procedure • • Compress the fingertip until it blanches. Release the pressure. Positive Sign Delayed capillary refill (>2 seconds). Indicates Reduced peripheral circulation. 3. FIGURE-OF-8 MEASUREMENT Purpose To assess hand and wrist swelling (edema). Procedure • Use a measuring tape in a standardized figure-of-8 pattern around the wrist and hand. Record the measurement. 1 • Positive Finding Increased measurement compared with the opposite side. Indicates Hand or wrist edema. 4. HAND VOLUME TEST Purpose To assess hand edema. Procedure • • Immerse the hand into a volumeter filled with water. Measure the volume of displaced water. Positive Finding Increased displaced volume. Indicates Hand swelling/edema. II. TESTS FOR THORACIC OUTLET SYNDROME 1. ADSON'S TEST Purpose To assess Thoracic Outlet Syndrome (TOS). Procedure • • Palpate the radial pulse. Patient extends the neck, rotates the head toward the tested side, and takes a deep breath. Positive Sign Reduced/absent radial pulse or reproduction of symptoms. Indicates Thoracic Outlet Syndrome. 2. ROOS STRESS TEST (EAST) Purpose To assess Thoracic Outlet Syndrome. Procedure Patient abducts shoulders to 90°, externally rotates them, and flexes elbows to 90°. 2 • Patient repeatedly opens and closes the hands for 3 minutes. Positive Sign Pain, numbness, tingling, heaviness, or inability to continue. Indicates Thoracic Outlet Syndrome.

**SPECIAL TESTS** --- **I TESTS FOR CIRCULATION and SWELLING** **1 ALLEN TEST** **Purpose** To assess the patency of the radial and ulnar arteries **Procedure** • • • • Patient clenches the fist Compress both radial and ulnar arteries Patient opens the hand Release one artery at a time and observe colour return **Positive Sign** Delayed or absent return of normal hand colour **Indicates** Arterial insufficiency or vascular occlusion **2 DIGITAL BLOOD FLOW TEST** **Purpose** To assess circulation to the fingers **Procedure** • • Compress the fingertip until it blanches Release the pressure **Positive Sign** Delayed capillary refill greater than 2 seconds **Indicates** Reduced peripheral circulation **3 FIGURE OF 8 MEASUREMENT** **Purpose** To assess hand and wrist swelling edema **Procedure** • Use a measuring tape in a standardized figure of 8 pattern around the wrist and hand Record the measurement 1 • **Positive Finding** Increased measurement compared with the opposite side **Indicates** Hand or wrist edema **4 HAND VOLUME TEST** **Purpose** To assess hand edema **Procedure** • • Immerse the hand into a volumeter filled with water Measure the volume of displaced water **Positive Finding** Increased displaced volume **Indicates** Hand swelling/edema --- **II TESTS FOR THORACIC OUTLET SYNDROME** **1 ADSONS TEST** **Purpose** To assess Thoracic Outlet Syndrome TOS **Procedure** • • Palpate the radial pulse Patient extends the neck rotates the head toward the tested side and takes a deep breath **Positive Sign** Reduced/absent radial pulse or reproduction of symptoms **Indicates** Thoracic Outlet Syndrome **2 ROOS STRESS TEST EAST** **Purpose** To assess Thoracic Outlet Syndrome **Procedure** Patient abducts shoulders to 90 degrees externally rotates them and flexes elbows to 90 degrees 2 • Patient repeatedly opens and closes the hands for 3 minutes **Positive Sign** Pain numbness tingling heaviness or inability to continue **Indicates** Thoracic Outlet Syndrome

Assessment - Demographic Data. Chief complaint, History of Present illness including functional limitation, Past Medical Surgical history, Family history, Occupational & Personal history, Socio-Economic, Environmental and Psychological History, Drug and Treatment History etc. Musculoskeletal Disorders:Vital parameters (Temperature, Respiratory Rate, Peripheral Pulses, Blood Pressure). Body Built of Patient, Artitude of Limb, Bony contours, Deformities, Trophic changes.
Examination of Posture. Examination of Spasm, Tenderness, Edema /swelling/wasting including Limb girth measurement. Scar examination. Motor Examination: Range of Motion, Joint Play & End Feel, Muscle Strength, Power, Endurance, Flexibility, Isometric Resisted Testing, Limb-length discrepancy, Pelvic inclination, Examination of Balance, Gait & External aids/assistive Device used etc. Sensory Examination: Superficial & Deep sensation, Reflexes, Dermatomes, and Myotomes etc.((Dermatomes
Definition: Areas of skin supplied by sensory fibers from a single spinal nerve root.
Purpose: Helps identify sensory loss due to nerve root lesions.
Examples:
C6: Thumb.
C7: Middle finger.
C8: Little finger.
L4: Medial side of leg.
L5: Dorsum of foot and great toe.
S1: Lateral side of foot.
Normal: Sensation is intact in the tested dermatome.
5. Myotomes
Definition: Groups of muscles predominantly supplied by a single spinal nerve root.
Purpose: Assesses motor function of spinal nerve roots.
Examples:
C5: Shoulder abduction.
C6: Wrist extension.
C7: Elbow extension.
C8: Finger flexion.
L2: Hip flexion.
L3: Knee extension.
L4: Ankle dorsiflexion.
L5: Great toe extension.
S1: Ankle plantarflexion.
Normal: Full strength, usually 5/5 on MMT.)) ((Assessment of Hand Function
1. Hand Pinches
Definition: Holding an object between the thumb and fingers.
Types: Tip-to-tip, lateral pinch, three-jaw chuck pinch.
Assessment: Pinch gauge.
Example: Holding a key or picking up a coin.
2. Hand Grip
Definition: Ability to grasp and hold ob
<truncated 1550 bytes>
 in young adults but can indicate heart failure.
S4: May occur with reduced ventricular compliance.
Normal breath sounds:
Vesicular: Soft, low-pitched sounds over most lung fields.
Bronchial: Heard normally over the trachea.
Bronchovesicular: Heard near the major bronchi.
Abnormal breath sounds:
Crackles: Popping sounds, e.g., pulmonary edema or pneumonia.
Wheeze: Musical sound, e.g., asthma.
Rhonchi: Low-pitched sounds due to airway secretions.
Stridor: Harsh sound due to upper airway obstruction.
Reduced or absent breath sounds: May occur with pneumothorax or pleural effusion.
2. Chest Expansion
Definition: Measurement of the increase in chest circumference during inspiration.
Method: Measure chest circumference during maximal expiration and inspiration using a measuring tape.
Common sites: Axillary, nipple line and xiphisternal level.
Normal: Approximately 3–5 cm in healthy adults, depending on age and measurement site.
Example: Reduced chest expansion in COPD.
3. Symmetry of Chest Movement
Definition: Assessment of equal movement of both sides of the chest during breathing.
Method: Observe chest movement or place hands symmetrically over the chest.
Normal: Both sides expand equally during inspiration.
Abnormal: Unequal chest expansion.
Example: Reduced movement on one side in pleural effusion or pneumothorax.
4. Body Mass Index (BMI)
Definition: Measurement of body weight in relation to height.
Formula: BMI = Weight (kg) ÷ Height (m²).
Normal adult BMI (WHO): 18.5–24.9 kg/m².
Example: Weight 60 kg, height 1.65 m, BMI = 22.0 kg/m².
5. Waist–Hip Ratio (WHR)
Definition: Ratio of waist circumference to hip circumference.
Formula: WHR = Waist circumference ÷ Hip circumference.
Normal reference values (WHO):
Men: ≤0.90
Women: ≤0.85
Example: Waist 80 cm, hip 100 cm, WHR = 0.80.
Purpose: Assesses abdominal fat distribution and associated health risk.))

### 🧑 You
<USER_REQUEST>
1. Neck Disability Index (NDI)
• Definition and Overview
• Developed by Howard Vernon and Silvano Mior in 1991 as a modification of the Oswestry Low Back Pain Disability Questionnaire.
• It is a patient-reported, condition-specific outcome measure designed to quantify self-rated disability and functional limitation in patients experiencing acute, subacute, or chronic neck pain (including cervical spondylosis, radiculopathy, and whiplash-associated disorders).

• Structure and Clinical Domains
• Comprises 10 itemized sections evaluating physical daily activities and symptom impact:
• Pain Intensity (severity of baseline cervical aching/pain)
• Personal Care (washing, dressing, grooming without exacerbating symptoms)
• Lifting (handling light, medium, or heavy objects)
• Reading (sustained cervical flexion/postural tolerance)
• Headaches (frequency and duration of cervicogenic headaches)
• Concentration (ability to focus on cognitive tasks)
• Work (ability to perform occupational duties)
• Driving (head turning, blind-spot checks, postural endurance)
• Sleeping (sleep disruption due to neck symptoms)
• Recreation (participation in hobbies, sports, and leisure)

• Scoring Methodology and Formula
• Each of the 10 sections is rated on a 6-point ordinal scale ranging from 0 (no limitation/no pain) to 5 (complete limitation/worst pain).
• Maximum possible raw score = 50 (when all 10 sections are completed).
• If all 10 sections are answered:
• Percentage Disability = (Total Raw Score / 50) x 100
• Handling missing sections (e.g., patient does not drive):
• Minimum completed sections required for validity: 9 out of 10.
• Percentage Disability = (Total Raw Score / (5 x Number of completed sections)) x 100

• Score Interpretation
• 0 to 4 points (0% to 8%): No disability
• 5 to 14 points (10% to 28%): Mild functional disability
• 15 to 24 points (30% to 48%): Moderate functional disability
• 25 to 34 points (50% to 68%): Severe functional disability
•
<truncated 7194 bytes>
alculation
• Each section contains 6 statements scored from 0 to 5:
• 0 indicates the least limitation or no pain.
• 5 indicates the greatest functional restriction or maximum pain.
• Maximum possible raw score = 50 (if all 10 sections are completed).
• Calculation when all sections are answered:
• ODI Percentage (%) = (Total Raw Score / 50) x 100
• Calculation when Section 8 (Sex Life) is omitted:
• ODI Percentage (%) = (Total Raw Score / 45) x 100

• Score Interpretation and Clinical Grading
• 0% to 20% (Minimal Disability): The patient can cope with most living activities. Treatment usually centers on ergonomics, core stabilization, and general fitness.
• 21% to 40% (Moderate Disability): The patient experiences pain with sitting, lifting, and standing. Conservative management (manual therapy, electrotherapy, active directional preference exercises) is indicated.
• 41% to 60% (Severe Disability): Pain remains the main problem; activities of daily living are significantly disrupted. Requires detailed diagnostic evaluation and structured intervention.
• 61% to 80% (Crippled): Back pain impinges on all aspects of the patient's life. Intervention requires advanced pain management, multidisciplinary care, or surgical consultation.
• 81% to 100% (Bedbound or Symptom Exaggeration): These patients are either completely bedridden or exaggerating their symptoms (requires screening for non-organic signs/Waddell signs).

• Relevance in Physiotherapy Practice
• Establishes functional baseline and tracks progress over weeks of lumbar core strengthening, McKenzie extension, or motor control stabilization.
• Serves as standard legal and disability documentation for return-to-work evaluations and medicolegal reporting.

• Clinical Limitations
• Patients from conservative cultures frequently leave the "Sex Life" section blank, necessitating manual formula adjustments.
• Shows
</USER_REQUEST>

### 🧑 You

use you format of avoe only for thsi text : # Sports-Specific Assessment
## BPT 3rd Year | 5-Mark University Exam Answers

- Definition: Sports-specific assessment is the systematic evaluation of an athlete’s physical fitness, functional abilities and performance-related components required for a particular sport.
- Purpose:
  - To assess fitness and sports performance.
  - To identify strengths and weaknesses.
  - To detect physical limitations and injury risk factors.
  - To monitor training progress and recovery.
  - To help plan individualized sports training and rehabilitation.

## 1. Cardiovascular Endurance

- Definition: Ability of the cardiovascular and respiratory systems to supply oxygen to working muscles during prolonged exercise.
- Purpose: To assess aerobic fitness and endurance capacity.
- Assessment tests:
  - Cooper 12-minute run test.
  - 20-metre multistage shuttle run test (Beep test).
  - 6-minute walk test.
- Parameters: Distance covered, heart rate, respiratory rate and recovery time.
- Example: Beep test for football players.

## 2. Muscular Strength

- Definition: Ability of a muscle or muscle group to produce maximum force against resistance.
- Purpose: To assess muscle force required for sports activities.
- Assessment tests:
  - One-repetition maximum (1RM) test.
  - Handgrip dynamometer test.
  - Isometric strength testing.
- Parameters: Maximum force produced and side-to-side differences.
- Example: 1RM squat test for weightlifters.

## 3. Flexibility

- Definition: Ability of a joint to move through its available range of motion.
- Purpose: To assess joint mobility and muscle flexibility required for sports movements.
- Assessment tests:
  - Sit-and-reach test.
  - Goniometric measurement.
  - Straight leg raise test.
- Parameters: Range of motion and muscle length.
- Example: Sit-and-reach test for gymnasts.

## 4. Speed

- Definition: Ability to perform a movement or cover a distance in the shortest possible time.
- 
<truncated 709 bytes>
on: Ability to produce maximum force in minimum time. Power is the product of force and velocity.
- Purpose: To assess explosive muscular performance.
- Assessment tests:
  - Vertical jump test.
  - Standing broad jump test.
  - Medicine ball throw test.
- Parameters: Jump height, distance or throwing distance.
- Example: Vertical jump test for volleyball players.

## 7. Balance

- Definition: Ability to maintain the body's centre of mass over its base of support.
- Types: Static balance and dynamic balance.
- Purpose: To assess postural control and stability during sports activities.
- Assessment tests:
  - Single-leg stance test.
  - Y-Balance test.
  - Star Excursion Balance Test (SEBT).
- Parameters: Time maintained, reach distance and postural stability.
- Example: Y-Balance test for football players.

## 8. Coordination

- Definition: Ability to perform smooth, accurate and controlled movements using different body parts.
- Purpose: To assess neuromuscular control and the integration of movements.
- Assessment tests:
  - Alternate hand wall-toss test.
  - Finger-to-nose test.
  - Alternate hand-foot tapping test.
- Parameters: Accuracy, timing, rhythm and movement smoothness.
- Example: Wall-toss test for tennis players.

## 9. Reaction Time

- Definition: Time taken to respond to a stimulus after it is presented.
- Types: Simple reaction time and choice reaction time.
- Purpose: To assess the speed of response to visual or auditory stimuli.
- Assessment tests:
  - Ruler drop test.
  - Computerized reaction-time test.
  - Sport-specific stimulus-response test.
- Parameters: Time taken to respond, usually measured in milliseconds.
- Example: Reaction-time test for a sprinter responding to the starting signal.

## References

- ACSM’s Guidelines for Exercise Testing and Prescription.
- Physiology of Sport and Exercise (Kenney, Wilmore and Costill).

<USER_REQUEST>
**Types of Pain**

* **Somatic Pain:**
  * Arises from noxious stimulation of musculoskeletal, cutaneous, and deep somatic structures.
  * *Superficial Somatic:* Originates in skin or subcutaneous tissues; sharp, pricking, well-localized with clearly defined boundaries.
  * *Deep Somatic:* Originates in muscles, tendons, ligaments, periosteum, and joint capsules; dull, aching, poorly localized, and tends to refer to related sclerotomal structures.
* **Somatic Referred Pain:**
  * Pain perceived at a site distant from the actual primary site of somatic injury or dysfunction (e.g., cervical facet joint dysfunction referring into the shoulder girdle).
  * Follows an embryological segmental or sclerotomal pattern without mechanical irritation or compression of neural tissue.
  * Characterized by a diffuse, deep ache with no primary neurological signs (normal reflexes, sensation, and motor power).
* **Neurogenic (Neuropathic) Pain:**
  * Initiated or caused by a primary lesion, dysfunction, or mechanical irritation of the peripheral or central nervous system.
  * Described as sharp, shooting, lancinating, burning, stabbing, or electric shock-like sensations.
  * Follows discrete anatomical distributions (dermatomal tracts or peripheral nerve distributions) and frequently presents with positive or negative neurological symptoms (paresthesia, hyperalgesia, allodynia, hypesthesia, motor deficits).
* **Visceral Pain:**
  * Arises from the distension, ischemia, spasm, or inflammation of internal organs (thoracic, abdominal, or pelvic).
  * Poorly localized, deep, sickening, dull, and cramp-like.
  * Frequently accompanied by autonomic manifestations (nausea, diaphoresis, pallor, blood pressure changes) and visceral-somatic referral patterns (e.g., myocardial ischemia referring to the jaw/left arm; gallbladder referral to the right inferior scapular region).

**Subjective Assessment of Pain**

* **Location:**
  * Pinpointing the exact anatomical site and boundary using a standardized body chart; distinguishin
<truncated 3499 bytes>
* Measured with a millimeter ruler from the left anchor to the patient's mark; documented as a continuous value from 0 to 100 mm (or 0 to 10 cm). Highly sensitive to small clinical changes.
* **Numerical Rating Scale (N.R.S.):**
  * *Format:* An 11-point segmented numerical scale ranging from 0 to 10.
  * *Anchors:* 0 = "No pain", 1–3 = "Mild pain", 4–6 = "Moderate pain", 7–9 = "Severe pain", 10 = "Worst possible pain".
  * *Administration:* Administered either verbally ("On a scale from 0 to 10, where 0 is no pain and 10 is the worst pain imaginable...") or visually as a numbered line.
  * *Documentation:* Recorded directly as a discrete integer score (e.g., NRS: 6/10). Highly practical for rapid clinical workflows and routine reassessment.
* **McGill's Modified Questionnaire (Short-Form McGill Pain Questionnaire - SF-MPQ):**
  * *Format:* A multidimensional pain assessment instrument evaluating sensory, affective, and evaluative qualities rather than intensity alone.
  * *Sensory Component:* 11 sensory word descriptors (throbbing, shooting, stabbing, sharp, cramping, gnawing, hot-burning, aching, heavy, tender, splitting).
  * *Affective Component:* 4 affective word descriptors (tiring-exhausting, sickening, fearful, punishing-cruel).
  * *Scoring System:* Each of the 15 descriptors is rated by the patient on a 4-point intensity scale:
    * 0 = None
    * 1 = Mild
    * 2 = Moderate
    * 3 = Severe
  * *Indices Documented:*
    * *Sensory Pain Rating Index (S-PRI):* Sum of sensory items (0–33).
    * *Affective Pain Rating Index (A-PRI):* Sum of affective items (0–12).
    * *Total PRI:* Combined sum (0–45).
    * *Present Pain Intensity (PPI):* A 6-point categorical index (0 = No pain, 1 = Mild, 2 = Discomforting, 3 = Distressing, 4 = Horrible, 5 = Excruciating).
    * *Visual Analogue Scale (VAS):* Standard 100 mm line included alongside the questionnaire for overall pain intensity.

# Investigations for Musculoskeletal Disorders

**BPT 3rd Year | University Examination Notes**

## 1. Blood Investigations and Markers

### Definition
- Blood investigations are laboratory tests performed to identify inflammation, infection, autoimmune disorders, metabolic bone diseases and other abnormalities affecting the musculoskeletal system.

### A. Complete Blood Count (CBC)
- **Definition:** A blood test that measures red blood cells, white blood cells and platelets.
- **Purpose:**
  - To detect infection and inflammation.
  - To identify anemia associated with chronic diseases.
- **Important findings:**
  - Increased WBC count: May indicate infection.
  - Decreased hemoglobin: Indicates anemia.
  - Increased platelet count: May occur in inflammatory conditions.

### B. Erythrocyte Sedimentation Rate (ESR)
- **Definition:** A test that measures the rate at which red blood cells settle in a tube over one hour.
- **Purpose:**
  - To detect inflammation in the body.
  - To help assess inflammatory and infectious musculoskeletal disorders.
- **Increased ESR may be seen in:**
  - Rheumatoid arthritis.
  - Osteomyelitis.
  - Septic arthritis.
  - Polymyalgia rheumatica.

### C. C-Reactive Protein (CRP)
- **Definition:** A protein produced by the liver in response to inflammation.
- **Purpose:**
  - To detect acute inflammation.
  - To monitor disease activity and response to treatment.
- **Increased CRP may be seen in:**
  - Rheumatoid arthritis.
  - Septic arthritis.
  - Osteomyelitis.
  - Other infections and inflammatory conditions.

### D. Rheumatoid Factor (RF)
- **Definition:** An autoantibody commonly associated with rheumatoid arthritis.
- **Purpose:**
  - To support the diagnosis of rheumatoid arthritis.
- **Positive RF may be seen in:**
  - Rheumatoid arthritis.
  - Other autoimmune diseases.
  - Some chronic infections.
- **Note:** A positive RF alone does not confirm rheumatoid arthritis.

### E. Anti-Cyclic Citrullinated Peptide (Anti-CCP)
- **Definition:** An antibody test used to 
<truncated 4795 bytes>
etect bone marrow abnormalities.
  - To investigate soft-tissue tumors and infections.
- **Advantages:**
  - Excellent visualization of soft tissues.
  - No ionizing radiation.
  - Provides detailed images of muscles, ligaments, tendons and cartilage.
- **Limitations:**
  - Expensive and less readily available.
  - Takes longer than X-ray or CT.
  - Some implanted devices and metallic foreign bodies may make MRI unsuitable or require special precautions.

### D. Ultrasonography (USG)

- **Definition:** An imaging technique that uses high-frequency sound waves to produce images of body structures.
- **Principle:** Sound waves are transmitted into the body, and the reflected echoes are used to create images.
- **Indications:**
  - To assess tendon injuries.
  - To detect joint effusion.
  - To identify muscle tears.
  - To detect bursitis and soft-tissue swelling.
  - To guide joint aspiration and injections.
- **Advantages:**
  - No ionizing radiation.
  - Real-time imaging.
  - Allows dynamic assessment during movement.
  - Relatively inexpensive and portable.
- **Limitations:**
  - Highly dependent on operator skill.
  - Limited visualization of deep structures.
  - Cannot adequately assess bone marrow or structures hidden behind bone.

---

## 3. Summary of Imaging Investigations

| Investigation | Main use |
|---|---|
| X-ray | Fractures, dislocations, bone alignment |
| CT scan | Complex fractures and detailed bone imaging |
| MRI | Ligaments, tendons, cartilage, discs and bone marrow |
| USG | Tendons, muscles, effusions and dynamic assessment |

**Clinical importance in physiotherapy:**

- Helps identify the underlying musculoskeletal pathology.
- Assists in selecting appropriate physiotherapy interventions.
- Helps identify contraindications and precautions.
- Supports monitoring of disease progression and recovery.
- Assists in determining the need for referral or further medical evaluation.

</USER_REQUEST>

<USER_REQUEST>
## 3. Modified Medical Research Council (mMRC) Dyspnea Scale

- **Definition:**
  - The mMRC Dyspnea Scale is a subjective assessment tool used to grade breathlessness according to its effect on daily physical activities.

- **Purpose:**
  - To assess the severity of dyspnea in respiratory disorders.
  - To evaluate functional limitation during daily activities.
  - To monitor disease progression and rehabilitation outcomes.
  - Commonly used in patients with COPD and other chronic respiratory diseases.

- **Grades of Dyspnea:**
  - **Grade 0:** Breathlessness occurs only during strenuous exercise.
  - **Grade 1:** Breathlessness occurs when hurrying on level ground or walking up a slight hill.
  - **Grade 2:** Walks slower than people of the same age on level ground due to breathlessness or has to stop for breath while walking at own pace.
  - **Grade 3:** Stops for breath after walking approximately 100 meters or after a few minutes on level ground.
  - **Grade 4:** Too breathless to leave the house or becomes breathless while dressing or undressing.

- **Clinical Significance:**
  - Higher grades indicate greater functional limitation due to breathlessness.
  - Useful in assessing COPD severity and functional disability.
  - Helps evaluate changes in symptoms during pulmonary rehabilitation.

- **Interpretation:**
  - Grade 0–1 – Minimal functional limitation.
  - Grade 2 – Moderate functional limitation.
  - Grade 3–4 – Severe functional limitation.

Source: National Academies of Sciences, Engineering, and Medicine.
https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK593672/

---

## 4. New York Heart Association (NYHA) Functional Classification

- **Definition:**
  - The NYHA Functional Classification is a clinical assessment tool used to classify patients with cardiovascular disease according to the degree of limitation in physical activity caused by cardiac symptoms.

- **Purpose:**
  - To assess functional capacity in patients with heart disease.
  - To determine the severity of symptoms during
<truncated 53 bytes>
ical progress.
  - Commonly used in patients with heart failure.

- **Grades of NYHA Classification:**

- **Class I:**
  - No limitation of physical activity.
  - Ordinary physical activity does not cause undue fatigue, palpitation, dyspnea, or chest pain.

- **Class II:**
  - Slight limitation of physical activity.
  - Patient is comfortable at rest.
  - Ordinary physical activity causes fatigue, palpitation, dyspnea, or chest pain.

- **Class III:**
  - Marked limitation of physical activity.
  - Patient is comfortable at rest.
  - Less than ordinary physical activity causes fatigue, palpitation, dyspnea, or chest pain.

- **Class IV:**
  - Patient is unable to perform any physical activity without discomfort.
  - Symptoms may be present even at rest.
  - Any physical activity increases discomfort.

- **Clinical Significance:**
  - Helps assess the functional status of patients with cardiovascular disorders.
  - Useful in cardiac rehabilitation and exercise prescription.
  - Helps monitor improvement or deterioration in functional capacity.
  - Higher class indicates greater limitation in physical activity.

Source: American Heart Association.
https://www.heart.org/en/health-topics/heart-failure/what-is-heart-failure/classes-of-heart-failure

---

## 5. Difference Between mMRC and NYHA Classification

- **mMRC Scale:**
  - Primarily used to assess breathlessness-related functional limitation in respiratory disorders.
  - Consists of 5 grades: 0–4.
  - Focuses mainly on breathlessness during daily activities.

- **NYHA Classification:**
  - Primarily used to assess functional limitation in cardiovascular disorders, especially heart failure.
  - Consists of 4 classes: I–IV.
  - Focuses on symptoms such as dyspnea, fatigue, palpitation, and chest pain during physical activity.

--- **3 Modified Medical Research Council mMRC Dyspnea Scale** • **Definition** • The mMRC Dyspnea Scale is a subjective assessment tool used to grade breathlessness according to its effect on daily physical activities • **Purpose** • To assess the severity of dyspnea in respiratory disorders • To evaluate functional limitation during daily activities • To monitor disease progression and rehabilitation outcomes • Commonly used in patients with COPD and other chronic respiratory diseases • **Grades of Dyspnea** • **Grade 0** Breathlessness occurs only during strenuous exercise • **Grade 1** Breathlessness occurs when hurrying on level ground or walking up a slight hill • **Grade 2** Walks slower than people of the same age on level ground due to breathlessness or has to stop for breath while walking at own pace • **Grade 3** Stops for breath after walking approximately 100 meters or after a few minutes on level ground • **Grade 4** Too breathless to leave the house or becomes breathless while dressing or undressing • **Clinical Significance** • Higher grades indicate greater functional limitation due to breathlessness • Useful in assessing COPD severity and functional disability • Helps evaluate changes in symptoms during pulmonary rehabilitation • **Interpretation** • Grade 0 to 1 Minimal functional limitation • Grade 2 Moderate functional limitation • Grade 3 to 4 Severe functional limitation • Source National Academies of Sciences Engineering and Medicine • https //www ncbi nlm nih gov/books/NBK593672/ --- **4 New York Heart Association NYHA Functional Classification** • **Definition** • The NYHA Functional Classification is a clinical assessment tool used to classify patients with cardiovascular disease according to the degree of limitation in physical activity caused by cardiac symptoms • **Purpose** • To assess functional capacity in patients with heart disease • To determine the severity of symptoms during physical activity • To monitor the patients clinical progress • Commonly used in patients with heart failure • **Grades of NYHA Classification** • **Class I** • No limitation of physical activity • Ordinary physical activity does not cause undue fatigue palpitation dyspnea or chest pain • **Class II** • Slight limitation of physical activity • Patient is comfortable at rest • Ordinary physical activity causes fatigue palpitation dyspnea or chest pain • **Class III** • Marked limitation of physical activity • Patient is comfortable at rest • Less than ordinary physical activity causes fatigue palpitation dyspnea or chest pain • **Class IV** • Patient is unable to perform any physical activity without discomfort • Symptoms may be present even at rest • Any physical activity increases discomfort • **Clinical Significance** • Helps assess the functional status of patients with cardiovascular disorders • Useful in cardiac rehabilitation and exercise prescription • Helps monitor improvement or deterioration in functional capacity • Higher class indicates greater limitation in physical activity • Source American Heart Association • https //www heart org/en/health topics/heart failure/what is heart failure/classes of heart failure --- **5 Difference Between mMRC and NYHA Classification** • **mMRC Scale** • Primarily used to assess breathlessness related functional limitation in respiratory disorders • Consists of 5 grades 0 to 4 • Focuses mainly on breathlessness during daily activities • **NYHA Classification** • Primarily used to assess functional limitation in cardiovascular disorders especially heart failure • Consists of 4 classes I to IV • Focuses on symptoms such as dyspnea fatigue palpitation and chest pain during physical activity • Both scales are subjective and should be interpreted alongside clinical examination and other functional assessments

# Exercise Tolerance Tests and Maximal Exercise Testing Protocols

## 1. 12-Minute Walk Test (12MWT)

* **Definition:** The 12-Minute Walk Test is a submaximal exercise test used to assess functional exercise capacity by measuring the maximum distance a person can walk in 12 minutes.
* **Purpose:**

  * To assess cardiovascular and respiratory endurance.
  * To evaluate functional exercise capacity.
  * To monitor progress during pulmonary and cardiac rehabilitation.
* **Equipment:**

  * Measured walking track.
  * Stopwatch.
  * Measuring tape.
  * Pulse oximeter.
  * Heart rate monitor.
* **Procedure:**

  * Explain the test and demonstrate the walking technique.
  * Record baseline heart rate, blood pressure, respiratory rate, SpO₂ and perceived exertion.
  * Ask the patient to walk as far as possible for 12 minutes.
  * The patient may slow down, stop or rest if required.
  * Provide standardized encouragement.
  * Record the total distance covered in 12 minutes.
  * Monitor symptoms, heart rate and SpO₂ during and after the test.
* **Outcome measures:**

  * Total distance walked in meters.
  * Heart rate response.
  * Oxygen saturation.
  * Dyspnea and fatigue using the Borg scale.
* **Advantages:**

  * Simple, inexpensive and easy to perform.
  * Requires minimal equipment.
  * Useful for assessing functional capacity.
* **Limitations:**

  * Results are influenced by motivation, walking speed and encouragement.
  * Requires adequate space and patient ability to walk.
* **Clinical applications:**

  * COPD and other respiratory disorders.
  * Cardiac rehabilitation.
  * Assessment of functional limitations.

## 2. Shuttle Run Test

* **Definition:** The Shuttle Run Test is a field-based exercise test in which the individual runs or walks repeatedly between two marked points according to audio signals.
* **Purpose:**

  * To assess cardiovascular endurance and aerobic fitness.
  * To estimate maximal oxygen uptake (VO₂ max).
  * To

  * Increasing incline may cause significant lower-limb fatigue.
  * Requires adequate walking ability.
  * Different Balke modifications may produce different results.
* **Clinical applications:**

  * Assessment of maximal aerobic capacity.
  * Cardiopulmonary exercise testing.
  * Evaluation of exercise tolerance.

## 9. Comparison of Bruce, Modified Bruce and Balke Protocols

* **Bruce Protocol:**

  * Speed and incline increase every three minutes.
  * Has relatively large workload increments.
  * Used for individuals who can tolerate higher exercise intensity.
* **Modified Bruce Protocol:**

  * Includes additional low-intensity stages before the standard Bruce stages.
  * Provides a more gradual initial increase in workload.
  * Used for individuals with reduced exercise tolerance.
* **Balke Protocol:**

  * Maintains a relatively constant treadmill speed.
  * Progressively increases incline.
  * Provides gradual workload progression.

## 10. Safety Precautions During Maximal Exercise Testing

* Obtain relevant medical history and assess contraindications before testing.
* Record baseline heart rate, blood pressure and ECG when clinically indicated.
* Ensure appropriate supervision and emergency equipment during clinical stress testing.
* Monitor heart rate, blood pressure, ECG, symptoms and perceived exertion.
* Stop the test if the individual develops chest pain, severe breathlessness, dizziness, abnormal ECG changes, a concerning blood pressure response or other significant symptoms.
* Provide an appropriate recovery period and monitor the patient after exercise.

## References

* American Thoracic Society/American College of Chest Physicians. ATS/ACCP Statement on Cardiopulmonary Exercise Testing.
* Treadmill Stress Testing, StatPearls, NCBI Bookshelf.
* Cardiopulmonary Exercise Testing in Congenital Heart Disease: Equipment and Test Protocols.

--- **1 Neck Disability Index NDI** • **Definition and Overview** • Developed by Howard Vernon and Silvano Mior in 1991 as a modification of the Oswestry Low Back Pain Disability Questionnaire • It is a patient reported condition specific outcome measure designed to quantify self rated disability and functional limitation in patients experiencing acute subacute or chronic neck pain including cervical spondylosis radiculopathy and whiplash associated disorders • **Structure and Clinical Domains** • Comprises 10 itemized sections evaluating physical daily activities and symptom impact • Pain Intensity severity of baseline cervical aching/pain • Personal Care washing dressing grooming without exacerbating symptoms • Lifting handling light medium or heavy objects • Reading sustained cervical flexion/postural tolerance • Headaches frequency and duration of cervicogenic headaches • Concentration ability to focus on cognitive tasks • Work ability to perform occupational duties • Driving head turning blind spot checks postural endurance • Sleeping sleep disruption due to neck symptoms • Recreation participation in hobbies sports and leisure • **Scoring Methodology and Formula** • Each of the 10 sections is rated on a 6 point ordinal scale ranging from 0 no limitation/no pain to 5 complete limitation/worst pain • Maximum possible raw score equals 50 when all 10 sections are completed • If all 10 sections are answered • Handling missing sections eg patient does not drive • Minimum completed sections required for validity 9 out of 10 • **Score Interpretation** • 0 to 4 points 0 percent to 8 percent No disability • 5 to 14 points 10 percent to 28 percent Mild functional disability • 15 to 24 points 30 percent to 48 percent Moderate functional disability • 25 to 34 points 50 percent to 68 percent Severe functional disability • 35 to 50 points 70 percent to 100 percent Complete functional disability / bedbound • **Psychometric Properties** • Test retest reliability High

effect in young active patients it does not evaluate running jumping cutting or athletic tasks • The physical function section contains 17 items that can be repetitive for individuals with mild early
